2.2.8.3(e) Excess expenditure on IAY display Board and Logo

As per para 5.10 of the guidelines, after construction of each dwelling unit under
IAY, a display board should be fixed indicating the Gol Rural Housing logo, year
of construction, name of the beneficiary etc. The expenditure on this account
could be met from the funds available under the scheme accruing due to interest.
The cost of each logo should not exceed हैं 30.

During test check of the records of JP Jagdalpur in Bastar district, it was observed
that the work of preparation of 1376 [AY board and logos along with photography
of houses constructed, was allotted to M/s IT Solutions, Jagdalpur. The work
order for the same was issued to the above firm at the rate of हैं 270 per unit. The
expenditure on photography was not permissible as per the guidelines and the
limit for expenditure on each IAY logo was only % 30. The preparation of boards
and logos at higher rates led to excess payment! "

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS while accepting the audit
observation stated that excess expenditure of € 2.59 lakh has since been recouped
from JP funds. It was further stated that instructions would be issued to incur
expenditure as per the norms.

Although the funds had been recouped, the loss of interest on LAY funds is
inevitable.

2.2.8.3(h) Withholding of assistance

According to para 3.2 of IAY guidelines 2004, if the beneficiary is unable to
construct smokeless chulha and toilet, deduction of 100 and हैं 600 respectively
would be made from the assistance. However, the provision for deduction was
withdrawn from the guidelines issued in the year 2010.

During the test check of records at selected ZPs and JPs, it was observed that the
JPs were following the practice of providing assistance in a staggered manner
depending on the stages of construction in accordance with para 4.10 of the IAY
guidelines. The first and second installment of assistance was transferred directly
to the bank account of the beneficiary, which could be withdrawn by the
beneficiary only on the authorization by JPs. In the sanction orders for assistance
pertaining to the period 2011-12 and 2012-13, it was being mentioned that the
i tance would be released only afier the construction of
chulha and toilet. Amount ranging from हैं 5,000 to = 7,000 was
earmarked as the last installment which would be withdrawn by the beneficiary
only after the construction of toilet and smokeless chulha.

It is evident from the above that although Gol had withdrawn the condition of
deduction for non-construction of smokeless chulha and toilet, Department was
following the practice of earmarking हैं 5,000 to ₹ 7,000 for the same. It was
further observed during the beneficiary survey that the beneficiaries had to
complete the construction work by raising funds through debt, thereby worsening
their financial condition.

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS accepted the audit
observation and stated that directions would be issued to all ZPs/JPs.

onvergence of IAY

The convergence of the IAY activities with other programmes is envisaged for
availability of a complete functional dwelling unit.

As per para 5.11 of the guidelines, the DRDAs are to make concerted efforts to
identify the programmes/schemes being implemented by various
Ministries/Departments of the Central Government which could be dovetailed
with IAY, so as to ensure that [AY beneficiaries also derive the benefits of these
schemes intended for rural BPL households.

2.2.8.4 (a) Lack of co-ordination for providing sanitary latrine to IAY
beneficiaries

As per para 5.11(ii) of IAY guidelines, there should be convergence with

activities and funds provided under the Total Sanitation Campaign (TSC) for

providing sanitary latrines in the [AY houses. An [AY beneficiary who would

construct a sanitary latrine would be eligible for financial assistance under TSC in

addition to the unit assistance he/she has got under JAY.

The beneficiary survey revealed that out of 1543 beneficiaries, 844 were not
having toilets and out of the 699 beneficiaries who have constructed toilets, only
93 have been provided the benefit of TSC. Scrutiny of the records of the test
checked ZPs and 15 JPs revealed that there was lack of convergence in large
number of cases. There was lack of efforts on the part of the implementing

2.2.8.4(b) Loans under Differential Rate of Interest scheme for IAY
beneficiaries

Paragraph 3.3 of IAY guidelines envisaged that in addition to the assistance
provided under the TAY, an TAY beneficiary can avail himself of a loan of upto
& 20,000 per housing unit under differential rate of interest (DRL) scheme at an
interest rate of four per cent per annum. It will be the responsibility of the State
Governments/DRDAs concerned to coordinate with the financial institutions to
make available the credit facility to the interested beneficiaries.

MoRD, Gol has also urged the State Government (May 2009) to create
widespread awareness of DRI benefits such that at least 25 per cent of the [AY
beneficiaries would be able to avail the benefit.

2.2.8.4(c) Drinking Water Supply and Environmental Improvements and Social
Forestry

As per the para 5.11 of guidelines, TAY households should be provided with
drinking water facilities through convergence with National Rural Water Supply
Programme (NRWSP) to provide every rural person with adequate water for
drinking, cooking and other domestic basic needs on a sustainable basis. Further,
pata 5.6 envisages plantation of trees in the entire habitation or around the
individual houses to enable the beneficiaries to source fuel/fodder/small timber in
due course. Cultivation of fruits and vegetables at household level in the houses
may also be encouraged for improving nutritional status.

During the survey conducted by audit, it was noticed that LAY households were
not provided the facility of convergence through NRWSP. However, the
households were having drinking water facilities nearby their residences by means
of community borewells. Except for the already available plantation or the
cultivations in the surroundings of the IAY households planted by the
beneficiaries themselves at their own expense and efforts, no other initiation of
plantation/cultivation was taken by the Department except in case of the cluster of
houses constructed at GP Charoda in JP Dharsiwa as discussed in paragraph
2.2.8.4(d).

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS accepted the audit
observation and stated that the matter would be taken up with Public Health
Engineering Department (PHED) and Horticulture departments.

2.2.8.4(e) Lack of initiatives to popularize the benefit of Credit-cum-Subsidy

According to para 3.4 of guidelines on Credit-cum-Subsidy for
construction/upgradation of rural houses, it was envisaged that upto 20 per cent of
the total funds can be utilized for upgradation of existing kutcha houses and
toward subsidy for construction of houses.

It was observed during audit that none of the IAY beneficiaries had availed the
benefit of Credit-cum-Subsidy. During the beneficiary survey, it was observed
that out of 1543 beneficiaries surveyed, 1520 were not aware of this benefit under
the scheme. Further, it was observed that no initiatives were made by the
Department to popularize the benefits of the scheme.

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS accepted the audit
observation and stated that steps would be taken to popularize the scheme.

The above reply confirms that there was lack of initiative by the State
Government for popularizing the scheme among the beneficiaries.

2.2.8.5(a) Popularisation of new and innovative Construction Technologies

According to para 5.1 of IAY guidelines, neither any contractor should be
involved in the construction by the ZP/DRDA/implementing agency, nor the

houses should be constructed by any Government Department. Government

It was observed in audit that although no external agency or any Government
agency was involved in the construction of [AY houses, no initiatives were taken

Involvement of NGOs

As per para 5.8 of the guidelines, local Non-Government Organisations (NGOs)
with proven good track records, wherever available, may be associated for
assistance in construction of dwelling units, along with supervision, guidance and
monitoring. In particular, NGOs should be used to popularize the use of sanitary
latrine and smokeless chulhas, as well as innovative technologies, materials
designs etc. for cost effective construction.

During audit of the selected ZPs and JPs, involvement of NGOs was not noticed,
although the guidelines suggested the same.

On this being pointed out, the Department stated that none of the NGOs in the
State had come forward to extend their involvement. Till 2013-14, there was no
separate fund earmarked for engaging NGOs. Letters have been issued to all the
CEO of ZPs in this regard. It was further stated that beneficiaries were to
construct the houses on their own and established PRIs existed for assistance,
therefore involvement of NGOs was not required.

During the exit conference (January 2014), ACS stated that there was lack of
good NGOs in the State.

The construction of [AY houses by Self Help Groups of beneficiaries themselves
as discussed in paragraph 2.2.8.4(d) would be a good initiative.

Further, as per para 5.9 of the guidelines, the implementing agencies should have
a complete inventory of houses constructed/upgraded under the TAY, giving
details of the date of start and the date of completion of construction of dwelling
unit, name of the village and block in which the house is located, occupation and
category of beneficiaries and other relevant particulars. Further, para 5.10 of the
guidelines envisages that completion of a dwelling unit in no case should take
more than two years.

Tn the selected 15 JPs, it was observed that none of the JPs were maintaining a
complete inventory of houses constructed under the TAY giving details of the date
of start and the date of completion of dwelling unit, name of the village and block
in which the house is located, occupation and category of beneficiaries and other
relevant particulars. Due to absence of such details, it was difficult for the JPs to
ensure the completion of IAY houses in the stipulated period.

During the scrutiny of records of the selected JPs, it was observed that due to lack
of proper inventory of houses and monitoring, instances of non-completion of
houses within stipulated period, delay in release of second installment, non-
commencement of construction work even after receipt of assistance were noticed
as discussed below:-

where the beneficiaries had not completed the construction work in the stipulated

period of two years.

provided was recovered when the case was noticed by JP in the year 2012-13.

Reply of CEO indicates that due to lack of proper monitoring, the amount of
assistance remained with the beneficiary for more than two years. No
documentary evidence was furnished in support of recovery of assistance.

2.2.8.5(¢) Lack of monitoring and evaluation in implementation of IAY
scheme

Para 6.1 and 6.2 of the [AY guidelines prescribe monitoring and evaluation
procedure to be followed for implementation of the [AY scheme along with
instructions for compliance with the same.

The monitoring at the State Level is the responsibility of State Level Vigilance
and Monitoring Committee” (VMC) for Rural Development Programmes. A
representative or nominee of the MoRD, Gol should invariably be invited to
participate in the meetings of the Committee.

During audit of the selected ZPs and JPs, it was noticed that except for the
monthly progress reports of physical and financial performance of IAY, no other
periodical reports were forwarded to the State level office from the ZPs and JPs
Further, the field officers at the JP level monitored the construction of [AY
houses by the beneficiaries through visits to the GPs but no schedule was fixed
for the same. No such field visit of higher officers was noticed. VMC meetings
were held at the State and district level for evaluation of all schemes and no
separate meeting was held for IAY. However, no representative of MoRD, Gol
was present in the State level VMC meetings. Intimation by the Department to
MoRD, Gol for nomination in the meetings of monitoring committee was also not
available on record.

It was noticed that meetings of monitoring committee was held at the ZP level
under the Chairmanship of the concerned Member of Parliament along with the
review of other schemes run by the Panchayat and Rural Department. Social audit
was not initiated for IAY.

On this being pointed out, the Department stated that no separate VMC meeting
was held for IAY at ZP level, rather it was held to cover all the schemes of
MoRD. All development schemes are reviewed in Gram Sabhas. However, social
audit would be mandatory for [AY from the year 2013-14. Officials of the
Department conduct field visit from time to time to oversee and monitor all the
schemes being operated in rural areas and report the findings, which are regularly
acted upon. However, no format had been prescribed for the same.

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS accepted the audit
observation and stated that appropriate directions would be issued.

Documentation of monitoring and its reporting would be helpful for initiating
remedial action on the shortcomings noticed.

2.2.8.5(e) Grievance Redressal Mechanism

Grievance redressal mechanism is an important tool for monitoring, evaluation
and effective implementation of any scheme. Para 6.1.2 of the guidelines
envisages to deal with complaints and as per this an effective Complaint
Monitoring System with adequate staff should be set up at the State level which
can visit the field, independent of the regular execution wing, and give a report to
the implementing agencies about the short-comings/shortfalls, for effective
redressal. The website of the Ministry has interactive provisions for filing
complaints. All Inspection reports should be hosted on the website.

It was observed that no specific guidelines or mechanism was in place for
redressal of grievances at any level throughout the Department to facilitate easy
lodging of complaints by the beneficiaries or eligible BPL families.

On this being pointed out, the Department stated that there was no separate
complaint cell for IAY. Awaas Soft is not very much functional for complaint
updation status.

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS stated that a grievance
redressal mechanism would be setup. It was further stated that [AY has been
included in Social Audit.

Chapter 2:Performance Audit

2.2.8.5(f) Non-updation of data in Awaas Soft

Awaas Soft is a web enabled software for TAY launched by MoRD in July 2010.
‘The Awaas Soft data ‘It 5 4 management information system (MIS) to fa ate e-governance for the
was not updated. scheme. The system was prepared by National Informatics Centre (NIC) in
collaboration with the MoRD. The reporting of complete and comprehensive data
ranging from physical, financial targets and achievement, details of beneficiaries,
progress of houses sanctioned and funds released etc. at all levels of
implementation of the scheme is to be uploaded in Awaas Soft.

Scrutiny of progress report available in Awaas Soft and the monthly progress
reports (MPRs) available at the State level revealed that the data of MIS in Awaas
Soft was available from the year 2009-10 onwards. Difference was noticed
between almost all the data of MPR and MIS during the period 2009-13. It is
evident from this that the data in Awaas Soft had not been updated and thus
indicates that the MIS developed was not fully operational and the figures
available were not dependable. There was wide range of difference between MPR
and MIS data during the period from 2009-10 to 2012-13 as shown in
Appendix 2.2.8.

On this being pointed out, the Department while accepting the audit observation
stated that Awaas Soft is new and some fields in the software were not
responding, Further, wrong entry cannot be rectified by the State Government as
the control of server is with Gol. It was also stated that no dedicated manpower is
available for MIS data entry of IAY, which is being executed with the help of
staff deployed for data entry of MGNREGS.

While accepting the constraints of the Department, the availability of unreliable
data of the last four years in the MIS for public domain could not be justified. Had
the data of Awaas Soft been updated periodically, the records could have been
helpful for monitoring, maintenance of financial and physical data of beneficiaries
as discussed in paragraphs 2.2.8.1(a), 2.2.8.2 and 2.2.8.5(b).

During the exit conference (January 2014), the ACS accepted the fact and stated
that corrective steps would be taken,

The targets of Indira Awaas Yojna (IAY) during the period of Performance audit
was almost fully achieved by the Department. However, we noticed certain
shortcomings during the Performance Audit. The beneficiary selection made by
the Gram Sabhas was not monitored properly at the JP level which led to
improper preparation of IAY waitlist. The [AY funds were not being kept in
separate bank accounts in JPs and the interest earned could not be bifurcated. The
interest accumulated on TAY funds in Zilla Panchayats was not being utilised.
There had been delay in release of State share. Instances of inadmissible
expenditure and diversion of funds were noticed. There was lack of convergence
of [AY with other schemes. The monitoring and evaluation needed further
strengthening, along with training to staff involved in implementation of the
scheme.

2.2.10 Recommendations

In the backdrop of Performance Audit, we recommend that:

© Preparation of Indira Awaas Yojana (IAY) waitlist and earmarking of
specified categories of beneficiaries should be in accordance with the
guidelines.

@ JAY finds should not be utilised for purposes other than providing
assistance under the scheme and separate bank accounts should be opened
for IAY funds.

@ State Government may ensure providing benefits to the targeted
beneficiaries by ensuring convergence with other schemes.

© Efforts should be made to popularise new and innovative techniques,
environment friendly materials, Differential Rate of Interest scheme and
Credit-cum-Subsidy scheme.

@ There should be proper monitoring of the scheme along with fixing of
norms regarding the periodicity and extent of inspections.

®@ Training should be imparted to the officials implementing the IAY scheme
and a grievance redressal mechanism should he established at the State
level.

The above recommendations were discussed with the Additional Chief
Secretary and he stated that instructions have been issued (December 2013) to
all the Chief Executive Officer, Zilla Panchayats to implement the
recommendations made by audit.

2.3 Review of the creation of infrastructure for National e-Governance Plan

Executive Summary

The National e-Governance Plan (NeGP) envisaged three pillar model! for delivery of
“web-enabled anytime, anywhere access” information and services in rural areas.
Government of Chhattisgarh (GoCG) has identified 10 key services for providing
Government to Citizen (G2C) services. The NeGP of Government of India (Gol) has a
clear focus on improving delivery of Government services to Citizens and
e-Governance is known to play a very active role in this endeavor. NeGP recognizes
that creation of Information Technology (IT) and services delivery infrastructure by the
State is essential for effective and cost efficient functioning of e-Governance.

The NeGP consists of three core projects- State Wide Area Network (SWAN), State
Data Centre (SDC) and Common Service Centers (CSCs). The total funds utilized for
SWAN was हैं 69.64 crore out of total allocated fund of 71.85 crore during 2005-13.
In SDC % 15.05 crore was utilised out of total allocated funds of € 18.93 crore while
fund utilisation for CSCs was हैं 17.73 crore out of total allocation of ¥ 23.93 crore.

The Performance Audit of the Department was conducted for the period 2005-13 to
examine the economy, efficiency and effectiveness of significant activities. The major
audit findings are highlighted below:
© Department failed to provide Government to Government (G2G) services through
SWAN even after incurring expenditure of हैं 69.64 crore and running parallel
network with SWAN connectivity led to extra financial burden on the State
Government.

available were utilized under SWAN, SDC and CSCs, respectively.

progress of implementation of the project.

2.3.1 Introduction

The Government of India approved the National e-Governance Plan (NeGP) in
May 2006 with the vision to “make all Government services accessible to the
common man in his locality, through common service delivery outlets and
ensure efficiency, transparency and reliability of such services at affordable
costs to realize the basic needs of the common man”. NeGP envisaged
bringing the different initiatives launched by different States/Departments
under a common umbrella to achieve the vision. NeGP was to be promoted as
a centralized initiative to the extent necessary to ensure citizen service
orientation and optimal use of technology but advocated a decentralized
implementation model.

The Government of Chhattisgarh (GoCG) appointed Chhattisgarh Infotech and
Biotech Promotion Society (CHIPS), a society under the control of the State
Government, as the State Designated Agency (SDA) for establishment and
implementation of NeGP. Being the representative of the State, CHiPS was
required to provide the necessary policy level support for regulation,
promotion and ramp up of NeGP and private sector services. The role of the
SDA was primarily to: facilitate e-readiness of the State, awareness
campaigns and the State Wide Area Network (SWAN) interface co-ordinate,
manage and monitor the receipt and utilisation of financial support received
from the State Government / Government of India (Gol), help identify
Common Service Centre (CSC) locations, co-ordinate and facilitate
interactions between the Service Centre Agencies (SCA) and other
Departments, coordinate and network with other concerned state level entities
and select SCA under the guidance of the High Level Committee/Apex
Committee.

Creation of core infrastructure in the form of SWAN, SDC, State Service
Delivery Gateway (SSDG) and support infrastructure of CSCs were identified
as important components that would ensure efficient delivery of e-services.

2.3.2 Audit Objectives

The audit objectives were to assess whether:

७ the planning, co-ordination and programme formulation for creation of
common/support infrastructure for NeGP were in line with the
Government's approach to e-Governance,

@ the Core Infrastructure (SWAN, SDC, SSDG) was planned and created to
facilitate effective implementation of NeGP,

@ =the Public-Private Partnership (PPP) model was implemented efficiently,
effectively and economically; and

@ the support infrastructure (CSCs) was planned and implemented to
promote NeGP effectively.


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

2.3.3 Audit Criteria

Criteria has been drawn from the following sources:
# IT Policy of the State Government.

@ Circulars issued and mandate documents prescribed by the
Government.

® Instructions of the Gol on implementation of e-governance schemes.

Scope of Audit and audit methodology

Review of creation of infrastructure for NeGP was conducted to cover the
activities undertaken by the State Information Technology (IT) Department
and Nodal agency CHiPS under NeGP during the period from 2005-06 to
2012-13. During the audit, economy and efficiency of the project
management in the formulation, implementation and operation of the core
infrastructure components associated with NeGP and its effectiveness in
achieving the Government’s vision were examined. The review also looked
into the utilisation of the core infrastructure by the State Governments/
departments for delivery of services.

The Audit objective, scope and methodology were discussed with the
Secretary, IT Department, Government of Chhattisgarh (GoCG) and Chief
Executive Officer, CHiPS in an entry conference held in September 2012.
Comprehensive assessment of CSC, SWAN and SDC was made between
September 2012-May 2013 through check of records of CHiPS, physical
verification of 43 CSCs of five Districts? (Appendix 2.3.1) 18 SWAN
locations in Raipur District and 10 key service Departments. Replies received
from CHIPS have been suitably incorporated whenever applicable.

2 ient features of the Programme

The e-Governance scheme broadly consists creation of the following three
major components:

# Core infrastructure (SWAN,SDC and SSDG)
# Mission Mode Projects (MMPs) - Back end.
७ The CSCs - Front end

The most important component in the e-Governance programme is the
creation of electronic service delivery platform through Common & Support
Infrastructure schemes and this was to be done through PPP model.

Government of Chhattisgarh identified 10 key services for providing to
common man through CSCs. When user requested to provide services, the
given information would be captured in the CSCs and this information will be
uploaded to SSDG through SWAN or other Internet connectivity. SSDG
provide unique ID for every request thereafter the data captured in the SDC
ate sent to the concemed Department, who will issue the certificate/provide
services after verification of uploaded data provided by the CSC.

Ten Key Service:-(i) Land records, (ii) Vehicle Registrati

(0) State Wide Area Network (SWAN)

SWAN is important for building the core infrastructure for supporting
e-Governance initiatives. The objective of the Scheme was to create a secured
Government network for the purpose of delivering Government to
Government (G2G) and Government to Citizen (G2C) services.

SWAN has two components (Vertical and Horizontal). The vertical
component is implemented by using three-tier architecture, State Headquarters
(SHQ"), District Headquarters (DHQ*) and Block Headquarters (BHQ*). Each
SHQ, DHQ and BHQ is called a Point of Presence (PoP’) and a bandwidth
aggregation point. In horizontal component, the Government departments at
each tier are connected to the respective PoPs. The bandwidth provision for
network connectivity is 2 Mbps up to the block level whereas the horizontal
component is connected with 512 Kbps.

(ii) State Data Centre (SDC)

SDC and SDA are located in the premises of SDA office building at Raipur
Chhattisgarh. SDC is important for supporting e-Governance initiatives. SDC
consolidates services, applications and infrastructure to provide efficient
electronic delivery of G2G, G2C and Government to Business (G2B) services.
Some of the key functionalities of SDC are Central Repository of the State,
Secure Data Storage, Online Delivery of Services, Citizen Information/
Services Portal, State Intranet Portal, Disaster Recovery, Remote Management
and Service Integration etc.

*SHQ- refers to the Point of Presence established at SHQ where all the WAN circuits from
DHQ are terminated.

5 DHQ. refers to the Point of Presence established at DHQ where all the WAN connections
from Blocks in the district and the SHQ are terminated.

° BHQ- refers to the Point of Presence established at the Block level and which also acts as an
entry point for the Government agencies located in the Block or village etc.

7 PoP- refers to Point of Presence with respect to SWAN setup in State.

70



Chapter 2:Performance Audit

(iii) State Service Delivery Gateway (SSDG)

Efficient e-Governance requires the State Governments to cooperate,
collaborate and integrate information across different departments in the State.
To simplify the above task, the concept of e-Governance Service Delivery
Gateways was conceptualized which will act as standards-based messaging
switch and provide seamless interoperability and exchange of data across the
departments. The functionality of the State Level Gateway is similar to the
National Service Delivery Gateway (NSDG) at the Centre and shall work peer
to peer with the Central NSDG and other Gateways. All the State services
shall be listed in this directory. The Gateway acts as the single point of access
to back end departments for all external entities. The State Level Gateway also
interacts with the NSDG at the Central level for exchange of data with Central
Mission Mode Projects (MMPs).

(iv) | Common Service Centres (CSCs)

The Common Service Centres (CSCs) are the strategic cornerstone of NeGP
The CSCs are to provide high quality & cost-effective video, voice & data
content services, Education, Health, Telemedicine, entertainment as well as
other private services. It is to offer web-enabled e-Governance services in
rural areas, including application forms, certificates and utility payments such
as electricity, telephone & water bills. The scheme aimed at providing citizen
centric services of the State and Central Governments in a convenient and
efficient manner across rural India with accountability and transparency.
All CSCs would have Broadband Internet enabled connectivity.

6 Financial Management

The component wise NeGP funds for CHiPS are received in two ways
(i) Department of Electronic and Information Technology (DEITY) share is
directly received by CHiPS and (ii) Additional Central Assistance (ACA) and
State Share fund are made available through State Budget. As per the
guidelines of DEITY, component wise bank account should be opened by
CHiPS and any interest received during the year in these accounts may be
adjusted in the same account.

The details of funds received and expenditure incurred under NeGP during the
period of audit are shown in Appendix 2.3.2

A review of the receipt and utilization of fund for the programme revealed the
followings:

2.3.6.1 Non utilization of scheme funds

CHiPS failed to utilize the funds received for SWAN scheme during 2005-11
due to delay in completion of site preparation work by Public Works
Department (PWD) and Final Acceptance Test (FAT*) with the BOOT
operator and the saving ranged between 82 and 100 per cent. However, the
fund utilization increased to 85 per cent in 2011-12 and 93 per cent in
2012-13. Further it was also observed that DEITY released the fund time to
time without ensuring the utilisation of funds.

*FAT- means the acceptance testing of the network including equipment at SHQ, all DHQ.
all BHQ and all co-located office.

2.3.6.2 Non release of State Share

The Administrative Approval of SWAN project for Chhattisgarh was accorded
for a total estimated cost of ¥ 91.27 crore. Out of this, the contribution of the
DEITY and the State Government in the form of Grants-in-aid was
% 51.25 crore and हैं 40.02 crore respectively. The grants were to be released to
CHiPS in 20 Quarterly Guaranteed Revenue (QGR) installments. State
Government share was required to be released from time to time in accordance
with DEITY share for quarterly payment to the BOOT operator.

During scrutiny of records it was noticed that though Gol released the central
share in time however State Government failed to release State share in due
time. Due to non release of the State Share, payment amounting to
& 8.28 crore could not be made to BOOT operator.

CHIPS stated that the State Share has been released up to 60 QGR. The
pending amount for audited QGRs (i.e. 7" to 10" QGR) will be released to the
operator as soon as the fund is received from Finance Department,
Government of Chhattisgarh

Chapter 2:Performance Audit

2.3.6.3 Short levy of penalty of 19.56 lakh

The Operation and Maintenance of CGSWAN was started from February
2010. The total project cost was हैं 80.02 crore'” which was to be released to
the BOOT operator in 20 installments in the form of QGR. As per the penalty
clause of the agreement, if the BOOT operator fails to provide the service to
the end user at each PoP, penalty is to be imposed as per calculation made by
TPA. The penalty is to be calculated on whole amount of the QGR for a
quarter.

During scrutiny of records, we found that while calculating the penalty for
inadequate availability of services and delay in implementation of the
CGSWAN, instead of calculating the penalty on whole amount of QGR of a
quarter, TPA calculated the penalty on 50 per cent of the QGR and the same
was passed for payment by CHiPS. As a result, penalty amounting to
₹ 19.56 lakh was levied against the leviable amount of हैं 39.12 lakh
(Appendix-2.3.3). This not only resulted in short levy of penalty but also led
to extension of undue financial aid to the BOOT operator.

CHiPS agreed (February 2014) with the audit observation and intimated that
the balance penalty amount will be adjusted in sixth QGR (for State share) and
11th QGR (for DEITY share).

2.3.6.4 Non submission of Utilisation Certificates

Administrative approval (March 2005) for = 8.16 crore was accorded by
DEITY for site preparation in 151 locations and floor plan for establishment
of CGSWAN with the instruction to utilize the funds only for the purpose for
which it was provided. Accordingly the civil work was executed (2007)
through PWD. An amount of % 3.88 crore was paid as advance to PWD.
Though the PWD completed 149 out of 151 works, utilisation certificates
(UC) for हैं 2.51 crore were not submitted till the date of audit (May 2013).
Despite this, no action was taken by the Department to obtain the UC.

2.3.6.5 Irregular release of revenue amounting to €43 lakh

2.3.6.6 Forfeited amount not deposited in Government account

The GoCG executed agreement (September 2008) with M/s Zoom Developers
to roll out 1898 CSCs in two zones. As per the agreement, SCA was to be set
up and operationalise CSCs at its own cost within 12 months. Howerver, the
company failed to install CSCs as per the agreement. Consequently agreement
was terminated (September 2011) and performance guarantee of & 1.28 crore
available with CHiPS was forfeited and kept in saving bank account. Since the
agreement was executed by GoCG and the establishment expenditure of
CHiPS and the expenditure related to CSCs was arranged by budgetary
provision of State Government and Gol, the forfeited amount should have
been deposited in Government Account immediately after realisation.

On this being pointed out, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that a letter has been
sent (January 2014) to Finance Department regarding guidance on forfeited
amount of performance Bank Guarantee from Zoom Developers Limited.

2.3.7 Implementation of core infrastructure projects

2.3.7.1 State Wide Area Network

The SWAN project was sanctioned (December 2005) for Chhattisgarh at a
total estimated cost of ¥ 91.27 crore and was to be implemented on PPP model
by CHiPS. Out of this, the contribution of the DEITY and the State
Government in the form of Grants-in-aid was 51.25 crore and ¥ 40.02 crore
respectively. The project was awarded to TCS on BOOT basis. An agreement
was entered into between CHiPS and TCS. As per the agreement, TCS was
required to install the vertical and horizontal PoPs by October 2008. In
bandwidth connectivity (vertical and horizontal) for e-Governance project
CHiPS and BSNL signed MoU (June 2009), Site preparation work was
entrusted to PWD in 2007 and an amount हैं 3.88 crore was paid as advance
During security of records, we observed that as against 3500 horizontal PoPs
and 151 vertical PoPs to be installed by October 2009, TCS installed
3212 horizontal PoPs and 149 vertical PoPs upto May 2013.

Scrutiny of the records of implementation of the SWAN project revealed
various deficiencies which are discussed below:-

(a) Delay in implementation of SWAN project

As per point no. 5 and 8 of the Schedule II of the agreement regarding
Acceptance Test and Implementation Schedule', TCS was responsible for
Supply, Installation, Implementation, Commissioning, Operations and
Maintenance of Chhattisgarh State Wide Area Network (CGSWAN) in the
State on BOOT basis for a period of five years from the date of functioning of
CGSWAN (February 2010). As per the agreement, the site preparation,
dispatch of network hardware to the site, implementation of the network and
FAT activity were to be done within the scheduled time. Any delay by the
concessionaire in the Final Acceptance Test (FAT) shall render the bidder
liable to appropriate Penalties.

Scrutiny of the records revealed that the implementation schedule given in the
agreement of the CGSWAN was not followed and the works were completed
with delay ranging from 14 to 59 weeks. Tt was seen from the Material Receipt
Note (MRN) that more than 120 (DHQ/BHQ) out of 149 locations, did not
receive hardware at the schedule time (i.e. hardware receipt between
November 2009 to November 2010). The delay in dispatch of hardware led to
delay in implementation of the network. Despite this, CHiPS did not impose
any penalty for delay, resulting in extension of undue financial benefit to the
BOOT operator.

CHIPS agreed with the audit observation and stated (February 2014) that site
preparation work was adversely affected due to the northern and southern part
of State under SWAN project being affected with Left Wing Extremism and
other reasons like non availability of electricity connection, limited manpower
of BOOT operator and non-availability of bandwidth provider.

(b) Delay in appointment of Third Party Auditor (TPA) tor CGSWAN
project

As per Para 2.1 of the guidelines, independent TPA should be appointed
before the FAT and commissioning of the SWAN for performance monitoring
of SWAN. The TPA would be appointed for a period of five years from the
date of FAT of the network.

Scrutiny of the records revealed that FAT was completed in February 2010
and the SWAN project was also commissioned in the same month. The FAT
certificate was also issued to the BOOT operator in February 2010. However,
it was observed that TPA (M/s Equant Technology Service India Ltd.) was
appointed in May 2011, after 15 months of issue of FAT certificate. Thus, due
to failure of CHiPS to timely appoint the TPA, FAT could not be conducted
but the certificate was issued to BOOT operator without successful testing by
TPA. Further, due to delay in appointment of TPA, the performance of the
project also could not be monitored between February 2010 and May 2011.

CHiPS agreed with the audit observation and stated (February 2014) that the
RFP for selection of TPA was not in shape at the time of CGSWAN bid
process. Though the appointment of TPA was delayed but as per DEITY
guidelines, the scope of TPA is to audit all the 20 QGR of the BOOT operator
and submit reports on various performance standards of the network.

(c) Status of Point of Presence (PoP)

As per the agreement, FAT would be conducted by STPI and CHiPS as per the
location of installation of equipment by TCS. The Operation and Maintenance
of the project was started from February 2010 and accordingly, TCS became
entitled for QGR payment

Scrutiny of the records of QGR payments revealed the followings:-

Further as per the Administrative approval (December 2005) of the CGSWAN
project, it was provided that a committee would take overall responsibility for
implementation of the SWAN project in the State and the committee may
meet at least once in a month to review the physical and financial progress of
the project.

During scrutiny of records, it was noticed that though the committee was
formed in March 2006 by GoCG, only eight meetings were held against
84 required during the last six years. Since the meetings were not conducted
regularly, the physical and financial progress of SWAN could not be reviewed
simultaneously by the committee. Due to non conducting regular SLAC
meeting, various issues like delay in project implementation, site preparation
work, non installation of 288 horizontal and two vertical PoPs and non
providing G2C services etc. were neither discussed nor suitable action were
taken to solve the above issue in the SLAC meeting. Thus the project was
run without proper monitoring during the entire period. For these reasons,
only 1618 out of 3500 horizontal PoPs were found working as per TPA, till
the date of audit (May 2013) as mentioned in para no.2.3.7.1 (d).

In reply CHéPS stated (February 2014) that the committee was formed to
monitor the progress of the Project and the meetings were scheduled as and
when required to review the progress of the project at a time as specified and
finalized by the members.

The reply is not acceptable because it was clearly mentioned in the
Administrative approval that the committee should meet at least once in a
month to review the physical and financial progress of the project. The non
convening of regular meetings resulted in improper monitoring of progress as
all the PoPs were not yet installed and also led to delay in implementation of
SWAN project.

SDC was envisaged to establish a robust infrastructure to enable the

The SDC, connected to the SWAN was to provide the access to the
e-Governance applications & services to Government employees through
Intranet and to the citizens through public Internet/CSCs etc.

The SDC project sanctioned (February 2008) for the State of Chhattisgarh at a
total estimated cost of ¥ 49.15 crore was to be implemented on PPP model by
CHiPS over a period of five years from the date of commencement. Out of
this cost DEITY contributed हैं 18.24 crore and the balance % 30.91 crore was
to be met through ACA for NeGP. The project implemented through M/s Sify
Ltd. and an agreement was signed between CHiPS and M/s Sify Ltd. in May
2012.

Scrutiny of records of implementation of SDC revealed the following:-
2.3.7.2 (i) Delays of 32 months in floating of tender for SDC

As per time schedule enclosed with the administrative approval, the RFP
should be released by the State within 13 weeks from the date of signing of
the agreement with the consultant. However, the tender was floated for
selecting the Implementing Agency (IA) only in May 2011, after delay of

The project was awarded to TCS in June 2012 for implementation at a cost of
र 9.29 crore.

With the implementation of SSDG, e-Forms application will enable citizens to
download forms and submit their applications electronically with help of
Electronic Forms hosted on the State Portal (SP) and routed by a common
services gateway (SSDG). This important initiative facilitating Electronic
Service Delivery will provide significant benefits to the citizens especially in
the form of a single gateway to citizen for service delivery. Development of
State Portal in Chhattisgarh was in progress as of May 2013

Scrutiny of records of implementation of SSDG revealed the following:-

2.3.7.4 Common Service Centres (CSC)

The CSC scheme under NeGP has been conceived as the front-end delivery
points for Government, private and social sector services to rural citizens of India,
in an integrated manner. The Scheme has a three tier implementation structure
comprising the State Designated Agency (SDA), Service Center Agency
(SCA) and the Village Level Entrepreneur (VLEs). The scheme envisaged
setting up of a High Level Committee which would oversee the
implementation of the scheme. State Government was required to create a
SDA which had definite roles to play in ensuring the success of the scheme.

The CSC project was sanctioned (November 2006) for the State at a total
estimated cost of % 53.68 crore and was to be implemented on PPP model by
CHIPS over a period of four years. The cost was to be equally shared by DEITY
and State Government. The contribution of DEITY was to be released as Grant-
in-Aid. The State was divided into four zones for implementation of the project.
The project for establishment of 3385 CSCs was awarded (September 2008) to
M/s AISECT for Durg and Korba zone”! and M/s Zoom Developer Ltd. for
Raipur and Raigarh Zone”.

As regards the targeted 3385 CSCs, 2460 CSCs were rolled out by SCA and
the remaining 925 CSCs were not rolled out till the date of audit (May 2013).

Test check of records of implementation of the CSCs revealed the following:-

2.3.7.4 (i) Determination of CSC locations and the role of State Designated
Agency (SDA) in identifying potential areas

As per RFP, CSCs were to be established through SCA within the overall
ceiling of one CSC in every six villages. The SCA would have to take the
concurrence of CHiPS and the District Collector before deciding the final
location preferences.

During scrutiny of records, it was revealed that CHiPS issued a letter to every
District Collector to provide a list of selected villages for establishing CSCs,
but no documentary evidences were available with CHIPS regarding receipt of
the list from the District Collectors except for Korba district and their
finalization of the location of CSCs on the basis of the list. During field
inspection, it was also observed that as per records, CSCs situated in remote
areas were either closed or not existed as discussed in subsequent para.

In reply, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that in the CSC implementation
guidelines, there is no specific direction for providing CSC location on the
SCAs. As the scheme is based on PPP mode, SCA had to identify potential
atea for the CSCs. However, CHiPS and IT Department had collected
proposed CSC locations from most of the District Administration after regular

2.3.7.4 (ii) Shortfall in the number of operational CSCs

As per RFP, the SCA would not be eligible for revenue support unless the
CSCs have been rolled out within the specified time frame and were certified
as operational by CHiPS.

During scrutiny of records it was noticed that

© Out of 3385, only 2460 CSCs (73 per cent) were established and physical
verification of only 60 CSCs (two per cent) was done by CHIPS till the
date of audit (May 2013).

@ Monthly report (September 2013) of Durg and Korba zone revealed that
as against the target 1487 CSCs, 1249 CSCs were installed by SCA
(M/s AISECT). However, the Online Monitoring Tool (OMT) showed that
only 1018 CSCs were installed.

@ Tt was also noticed that as per report (September 2012) of OMT, per day

report, 310 CSCs were shown connected through BSNL and 942 CSCs
with other connectivity.

© Duce to the termination (September 2011) of one SCA”, the status of 1200

(December 2013).

On this being pointed out, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that the non
installation of CSCs was mainly due to Left Wing Extremism, non availability
of basic infrastructure, unwillingness of local people to become VLEs, low
population density etc.

The reply is not acceptable as CHiPS failed to take these factors into account
while finalising the CSCs.

2.3.7.4 (iii) Irregular selection of Service Center Agency(SCA) for CSCs.

For implementation of CSCs in the State, CHiPS had selected AISECT for
Durg and Korba Zone and M/s Zoom Developers (P) Ltd. for Raipur and
Raigarh Zone as SCA through open bid process (September 2008). Due to
non adherence of Master Service Agreement (MSA) and non performance in
the project, the contract of M/s Zoom Developers (P) Ltd was terminated
(September 2011).

During scrutiny of records, it was noticed that after termination of one of the
SCA, the CHiPS did not take initiative to float tender and had directly
appointed SPV as a SCA for Raipur and Raigarh Zone without inviting tender
and also without taking approval of High Level Committee. Neither any
agreement was signed nor was any type of performance guarantee obtained by
CHiPS from this SPV. AISECT was made the implementing partner by the
SPV for Raipur and Raigarh Zone and the rates of previous SCA was taken as
the rate of revenue support

23 M/s Zoom Developer Ltd.

83


Lack of initiation
by CHIPS led to
non delivery of

Audit Report on General, Social and Economic (Non-PSUs) Sectors for the year ended 31 March 2013

In reply, CHIPS stated (February 2014) that after termination of contract with
M/s Zoom Developers limited the re-bid process was not done as after
termination of the SCA, only two year time period was left for implementation
CSCs and further stated that most of the district in these two zones are Left
Wing Extremists areas.

The fact remains that award of work without tendering was not only irregular,
it also denied healthy competition and transparency in the tendering process.

2.3.7.4 (iv) Non providing of Government to Citizen (G2C) services through
CSCs

(a) The NeGP envisaged three pillar model” for delivery of “web-enabled
anytime, anywhere access” information and services in rural areas. The role of
CSCs was envisaged as the front end delivery network for Government
services. Government of Chhattisgarh identified 10 key as mentioned foot
note two.

Scrutiny of records revealed that for providing G2C services as notified under
the Chhattisgarh Citizen Service (Electronic Governance) Rules 2003, these
services have been provided through Choice portal for which a Choice Agent
has to execute the agreement with the concerned District Collector with
security deposit of ¥ 20,000. GoCG declared the CSC as a Gramin Choice
Agent and it became mandatory to follow the above rule. Out of 2597 CSCs
established in the State, only eight CSCs had produced agreement copy to
CHiPS for registration in Choice portal which facilitated to provide G2C
services. The remaining 2589 CSCs had neither submitted the agreement nor
the CHiPS initiated any action for submission of the same by these CSCs.
Further, as against 10 key services to be provided though CSCs, only one
service (issue of certificates) was provided by eight CSCs and only 859
certificates were issued by the CSCs during last four years

In reply, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that electronic delivery of said
services is based on computerization of line departments. All such
departments have already been informed about CSC project and respective
departments have been requested to utilize these common infrastructures
being created by DEITY for providing G2C services. SSDG launched in the
month of October 2013 to deliver various citizen centric services. At present
services from Women and Child Development and Department of Commerce
and Industries are running.

The reply is not acceptable as CHiPS tailed to provide G2C services through
CSCs due to delay in implementation of core infrastructure projects i.e.
SWAN, SDC and SSDG and delay in digitization of departmental data.

(b) Scrutiny of information provided (February 2014) by the departments, it
was further revealed that out of 10 key Departments, Social Welfare
Department had not started G2C services, three departments viz. Food
Department, Public Grievance Department and Urban Development
Department did not furnish any information regarding IT enabled services.
The rest six departments have been providing G2C services and developed

* State Wide Area Network, State Data Centre and Common Service Centres

In absence of link with NeGP, the objectives of the NeGP scheme for
providing "web-enabled anytime, anywhere access" routed through common
services gateway (SSDG) could not be achieved.

2.3.7.4 (v) Unfruitful expenditure of % 4.47 crore under CSC project due to
lack of coordination between BSNL and CHiPS

Government of Chhattisgarh had decided to establish 3385 CSCs through
which the Government services were to be provided electronically. Of these,
2417 CSCs (71 per cent) were established and BSNL was authorized for
providing internet connectivity in these CSCs by Gol. The Detail Project
Report submitted by BSNL for connectivity at CSCs included the follows:

During scrutiny of records, it was noticed that for connectivity in CSCs,
40 BTS Towers were established (February 2010) by BSNL under Wi-Max
phase-I and CHiPS had released payment amounting to हैं 4.47 crore to BSNL
(October 2010). However CHiPS neither intimated the CSCs about the
location of these BTS tower nor made any correspondence with BSNL for
providing connectivity through Wi-Max.

As per BSNL records (December 2013), total 1042 CSCs were planned to be
connected and of which 694 CSCs were provided connection through 40 BTS
towers. However, those only 23 CSCs were actually connected (Durg district)
on Wi-Max. Thus it is clear that due to lack of co-ordination between BSNL
and CHIPS, the connectivity issue could not be resolved and this resulted in
unfruitful expenditure of = 4.47 crore.

Tn reply, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that though BSNL has utilized full
amount of ACA fund but still CSCs are not availing the facility of Wi-Max in
most of the location due to following reasons: Bill of Wi-Max connection on
higher side, Interrupted network connectivity of Wi-Max, Lack of awareness
of CSC scheme in the line offices of BSNL and unaffordable tariff of BSNL
Wi-Max.

The reply is not acceptable as CHIPS failed to ensure connectivity through
Wi-Max by properly coordinating with BSNL.

2.3.7.4 (vi) Discrepancies in the working of CSCs

During the test check of 43 CSCs functioning at different blocks under the
SCA in Raigarh, Dhamtari, Bilaspur, Raipur and Janjgir-Champa, the
following discrepancies were noticed:

@ = Out of the 43 CSCs test checked, only 28 CSCs were established and
found working and 15 CSCs were not in existence.

Thus due to lack of monitoring by CHiPS, all the CSCs could not be

CSCs.

2.3.7.4 (vii) Inadequate monitoring by the Apex committee on the
implementation of NeGP

As per the guidelines, GoCG declared (January 2007) Empowered Committee
as a State High Level Committee (HLC) to monitor the implementation of the
CSC project which was to meet at least once in a month to review the physical
and financial progress of the CSCs project. The committee was headed by
Chief Secretary and the Secretaries of various departments (i.e. Industries and
IT, PWD, Water Resource Department and Energy, Finance, School
Education, Panchayat and Rural Development, Public Relation etc) were the
members.

It was noticed that during the last seven years, 13 meetings of the Empowered
Committee were held and agenda on CSC was kept in five meetings only.
Many of the issues which could have been implemented only afier the
approval of the Empowered Committee like termination of contract of Zoom
Developer, status of rolled out, appointment of SPV as SCA, providing G2C
services through CSCs were neither included in the agenda item nor discussed
and implemented with the approval of the Empowered Comittee.

In reply, CHiPS stated (February 2014) that, the empowered committee is
headed by the Chief Secretary, GoCG and other committee members are
secretaries of various departments of the State. Therefore, it is not practical to
schedule the meeting every month. Based on the requirement it is scheduled.
The fact remains that meetings were not held regularly as prescribed in the
guidelines. The co-ordination of line Departments could not be established
and due to lack of coordination, CHiPS had failed to provide G2C and G2G

services.

86


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

2.3.8 Conclusion

Even after lapse of more than seven years since launch of NeGP, the
objectives of the scheme could not be achieved as successful establishment of
pre-requisites like [T core infrastructure remained unaccomplished and the
project could not be made operational. SWAN network could not be utilised
optimally in the absence of availability of G2C Services and incomplete rolled
out of CSCs in rural areas. Interconnectivity between various departments for
information sharing and service delivery also could not be achieved.
Non-verification and inspection of CSCs actually rolled out and made
functional was the result of lack of monitoring by High Level Committee.
Thus, the end use outcome viz, availability of ‘any time any where access to
Government services’ as envisaged in the NeGP guidelines could not be
achieved due to ineffective project planning, implementation and monitoring.

2.3.9 Recommendations

We recommend that:

@ Time bound implementation of the project through proper planning,
implementation and monitoring may be ensured.

@ Proper planning should be made to avoid overlapping of parallel
network connectivity.

© Optimum utilisation of funds provided for implementation of NeGP
may be ensured.

@ Guideline for implementation of NeGP should be properly followed.

@ All the records/data should be digitalized on priority basis.

Audit Report on General, Social and Economic (Non-PSUs) Sectors for the year ended 31 March

2.4.1 Introduction

The Public Works Department (PWD) is responsible for construction,
development and maintenance of roads and bridges such as State Highways
(SH), National Highways (NH), Major and Other District Roads and Rural
Roads under different schemes viz. Central Road Fund (CRF), Minimum
Needs Programme (MNP), Basic Minimum Needs Programme (BMNP) etc.
We in audit reviewed development of roads under CRF and MNP schemes.

Central Road Fund

The CRF Act came into effect from 27 December 2000. Under the Act, the
Central Government levies and collects cess, a duty of excise and customs for
the purpose of this Act, on every item specified in the schedule at specified
rates. The fund is utilised for the development and maintenance of NHs, State
Roads including roads of inter-State and economic importance excluding
Rural Roads. The portion of Fund allocated for expenditure in the various
States is retained by the Central Government until it is actually required for
expenditure. The Government of India (Gol) sanctioned (2001-2013)
development of roads having a length of 1528.22 km for % 505.60 crore to the
Government of Chhattisgarh (GoCG). Of this, development of 1279.52 km
was completed and remaining 248.70 km was in progress after incurring
expenditure of € 403.72 crore as of March 2013.

Minimum Needs Programme

The MNP a State sponsored scheme was introduced in the first year of the
Fifth Five Year Plan. The objective of the programme is to establish a network
of basic services and facilities of social consumption in all the areas up to
nationally accepted norms, within a specified time-frame. The programme is
designed to assist in raising living standards and in reducing the regional
disparities in development. Under this programme PWD, GoCG is responsible
for development of Rural Roads’. During 2008-13, the PWD targeted for
development of 5470 km road under this programme, against which 3725 km
was developed with expenditure of % 524.64 crore as of March 2013.

2.4.2 Organisational set-up

The PWD is headed by the Principal Secretary (PS) at Government level.
Engineer-in-Chief (E-in-C), PWD is the administrative head of the PWD and
is assisted by four’ Chief Engineers (CE), eight Superintending Engineers
(SE) and 35 Executive Engineers (EE) for implementation of projects. The
works are being executed through 35 divisions of PWD headed by the EEs.

2.4.3 Audit Objective

The Performance Audit was conducted to assess whether-

e Methodology for selection of roads was as per guidelines of the
programme and proper planning was done for execution of works;

e Fund management was efficient and effective; and

' The rural roads includes those roads which are not in the core-network of PMGSY and

are under the jurisdiction of PWD.

2. Ambikapur, Bilaspur, Jagdalpur and Raipur

e Execution of project was carried out economically, efficiently,
effectively and as per norms.

Audit criteria

The audit criteria was derived from the following sources :

e Central Road Fund (State Roads) Rules 2007 issued by Ministry of
Road Transport and Highways (MORT&H), Gol.

e Detailed Project Reports/Stage-[ estimates based on which
Administrative Approval (AA) accorded by Government;

e Technical Sanction, Detailed estimates, Notice _ Inviting
Tender/contract documents;

e Provisions of Works Department (WD) Manual and Schedule of Rates
(SORs);

e Specifications for Road and Bridge works issued by MORT&H and
Indian Road Congress (IRC); and

e Inspection reports of higher authorities.

2.4, udit scope and methodology

In Performance Audit, we examined (April-July 2013) records relating to
development of roads under CRF and MNP in the offices of E-in-C and
concemed Executing Divisions. For our detailed scrutiny, we reviewed the
records of seven divisions’ for MNP works, four divisions’ for both CRF and
MNP works and four divisions’ for CRF works. We in audit examined
10 works under CRF and 26 works under MNP executed by these divisions
during 2008-13.

An entry conference with the Principal Secretary (PS), PWD was held
(May 2013) to discuss the objective, scope and methodology of audit. The
audit findings were brought to the notice of the Government, however no reply
was furnished (January 2014). The exit conference was requested for on
27 January 2014 but could not be held due to non availability of head of
department. The replies as received from the EEs of the respective divisions
have been incorporated in the Performance Audit.

2.4.6 Planning and Implementation

The MORT&H, Gol issued (10 July 2007) Central Road Fund (State Roads)
Rules 2007 containing detailed guidelines for disbursement of funds from the
CRF. As per the Rules, for obtaining sanction under CRF, the State
Government should make available a list of projects to be taken up to the Gol
alongwith their cost estimates. The Gol shall identify the projects for release
of Funds in consultation with the State Governments. If, all the projects so
identified cannot be sanctioned due to shortage of fund, the Gol shall prioritise
the projects in consultation with the State Government.

these Specifications. The material shall be laid in one or more layers as necessary to lines,
grades and cross-sections shown on the approved drawings or as directed by the Engineer.

2.4.6.2 Works taken up without detailed survey

(i) We observed that construction of Nandghat-Bhatapara-Chandkhuri road
sanctioned for ~ 7.70 crore under CRF was awarded (August 2005) to a
contractor for % 8.31 crore. During execution, the quantity of earthwork was
increased. As a result the work was finally completed (June 2008) at a cost of
₹ 9.84 crore. Since the CRF guidelines allows for extra expenditure to the
extent of 10 per cent of the sanctioned amount, the State Government had to
bear the extra expenditure of € 1.37 crore”’.

In reply, the EE, PWD (B&R) Division Balodabazar stated (June 2013) that
cost increased due to execution of work according to requirement of actual site
condition.

The variation in quantities of earth work at post tender stage was also
indicative of inadequate survey and investigation before taking up the work.
Had the estimate been prepared accurately after detailed survey and
investigation, the entire amount could have been reimbursed by Gol and the
State fund could have been utilised on other State priorities.

(ii) The work of construction of Ambikapur Ring Road under MNP was
sanctioned (September 2006) for हैं 10.46 crore by GOCG, PWD. The estimate
included execution of the items of 20 mm OGPC and Seal Coat for Black
Topping (BT) work of the road. Work order for execution of the work was
issued (January 2007) by the EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Ambikapur to a
contractor at 19.80 per cent below SOR for completion within 18 months
(ie., July 2008). During inspection of the work, the E-in-C and CE, Bilaspur
observed that the specification provided in the agreement was not as per the
traffic density of the road and accordingly instructed (October 2007 and July
2008) for substituting the items of OGPC and Seal Coat with BM and SDBC.
Subsequently, the sanction was revised (August 2008) to = 13.08 crore. As the
contractor refused to execute the balance work as per revised estimate, the EE
foreclosed (August 2008) the contract and payment of % 4.51 crore was made
(January 2009) to the contractor.

We observed that after foreclosure of the contract, work order for the balance
work of % 7.82 crore was issued (June 2009) by the EE to another contractor at

2.4.6.3 Delay in completion due to execution of works without forest
clearance and land acquisition

The Forest (Conservation) Act, 1980 provides that prior approval of the Gol is
required for use of forest land for non-forest purposes. Gol further clarified
(March 1982) that diversion of forest land for non-forestry activities in
anticipation of approval is not permitted and no requests for ex-post facto
approval would be entertained.

Para 2.104 Note (3) of WD Manual stipulates that when the estimate is
sanctioned and funds allotted, an application for acquisition of land shall be
sent to the Collector by the authorities and the Notification for the acquisition
of land required for any particular work must be submitted before the work is
put in hand.

Scrutiny of records revealed execution of works without obtaining forest
clearance and land acquisition resulted in delay in completion of works and
avoidable expenditure as discussed in the subsequent paragraphs:

(a) Avoidable payment of price escalation of € 2.80 crore

The work of improvement and widening of Ratanpur-Majhwani-Kenda-
Keonchi road was sanctioned (July 2009) for = 38.18 crore under MNP. Since
the work also involved execution of work in Forest area, the EE, PWD (B&R)
Division, Pendra Road wrote (September 2009) to the DFO, Marwahi Division
for providing detailed survey report, which was submitted by the DFO in
December 2009 with the advice to submit detailed proposal for onward
submission to Gol. The detailed proposal was finally submitted
(19 April 2010) to the Chief Conservator of Forest-cum-Nodal Officer, Forest
Conservation Act, Chhattisgarh, Raipur by the EE and the clearance was
awaited (July 2013).

Scrutiny of records revealed that while submitting the proposal for technical
sanction, the EE informed that land with 80 feet was available in each km.
Further, notwithstanding to the provisions mentioned above and without
obtaining the forest clearance from Gol, tenders were invited (November
2009) and work order (8 April 2010) was issued to the contractor. As a result,

revenue department with PWD. Efforts are being made to finalise the land
acquisition case.

The fact remains that initial connectivity could not be provided due to
execution of work without acquisition of land, thereby rendering the
expenditure of 93.20 lakh become intfructuous

2.4.6.4 Execution of work beyond requirement resulted in avoidable
expenditure of % 1.40 crore

According to guidelines issued (March 2010) by MORT&H, Gol, the traffic
considered for design of road should be based on expected volume of traffic at
the end of design life of 10 to 15 years with the specified rate of traffic growth.
The volume of traffic is to be calculated in terms of numbers of Passenger Car
Units (PCU) for the purpose of width of the road. The capacity of road that
could cater to the traffic for the design life is as detailed below:

No. __Traftic volume Lane width Ty

ie Upto 4000 PCU | Single lane having 9 metre formation and 3.75 metre
carriage width
2, Above 4000 PCU ‘| Intermediate lane having 10 mt. formation and 5.50

metre carriage width.
Above 10000 PCU Double lane having 10 metre formation and seven metre
carriage width

we

As per survey (2008), the traffic census of Kanker-Bhanupratapput-Sambalpur
road” under CRF was 3119 PCU. Accordingly, the PWD designed the road
for 10 years life period excluding two years constructions period (2020) with
annual traffic growth of 7.5 per cent and anticipated PCU as 7428. As per
above guidelines, the required carriage width should be 5.5 mt for 7428 PCU.

We observed that as against the above, the road was however constructed with
cattiage width of seven metre. The execution of road work in excess of
1.5 metre width resulted in extra expenditure of = 1.40 crore as detailed in
Appendix-2.4.1.

In reply, the EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Bhanupratapur stated (June 2013) that
the decision regarding extra width was taken by higher authorities for
maintaining parity of road width with NH.

The reply confirms that the guidelines issued by MORT&H, Gol were not
complied and the road was widened beyond its requirement. Regarding parity
of road width with NH, no such proposal was made by State
Government to Gol.

2.4.7.2 Non remittance of royalty of = 1.31 crore to Government account

Clause-35 of the standard contract document stipulates that the contractor shall
pay all quarries royalty charges on account of use of minor minerals such as
sand, moorum, metal etc. If the contractor fails to produce the royalty
clearance certificate from the concerned department, then the EE shall deduct
the royalty charges from his bills and keep in deposit head, which shall be
refunded to the contractor on production of royalty clearance certificate from
the concerned department. If contractor fails to produce the same certificate
within 30 days of submission of final bill, then royalty charges which are kept
under the deposit head by the EE shall be deposited to the concerned
department head and contractor’s final bill payment shall be released.

It was observed that royalty amounting to हैं 1.31 crore deducted from the bills
of seven contractors was kept under Civil Deposits by the EEs of three
divisions” without remitting them into Government account even after lapse
of 19 to 32 months of payment of final bills.

On this being pointed out in audit the EE, PWD, Gariyaband remitted
(December 2013) an amount of हैं 17.04 lakh, which was after lapse of 15 to
25 months of their deductions. The EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Balodabazar
and Korba stated (June 2013) that royalty will be remitted as soon as allotment
is received.

The fact remains that an amount of % 1.14 crore pertaining to the EE, PWD
(B&R) Division, Balodabazar and Korba was still kept under Civil Deposits
(December 2013).

2.4.8.3 Execution of dense bituminous macadam/bituminous macadam
beyond required design resulted in superfluous expenditure

As per IRC 37, crust design of the road should be prepared on the basis of
CBR value and traffic density of the road.

During scrutiny of records, it was observed that three roads* were executed
under CRF and MNP, for which estimates were prepared in accordance with
IRC 37. As per sanctioned estimate, one layer of BM/DBM with profile
corrective course with an average thickness of 53-56 mm was to be laid on
freshly laid WBM/WMM. However, in actual execution, thickness of
BM/DBM was enhanced to average thickness of 71-74 mm from the
sanctioned thickness. No reasons were found on record regarding execution of
BM/DBM with extra thickness. This had resulted in extra expenditure of
र 3.30 crore (Appendix-2.4.3).

In reply, the EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Ambikapur stated (June 2013) that
thickness of BM was enhanced as the item of BUSG which was to be used for
levelling course was not executed. Further, EE, PWD (B&R) Division,
Manendragath stated (November 2012) that the extra thickness in execution of
DBM was provided for maintaining profile correction and for camber purpose.

The reply of EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Ambikapur is not acceptable because
provision of BUSG as stated was not provided for in the estimate and the

work valuing = 23.44 lakh only against the contract amount of हैं 2.43 crore.
Since the progress of the work was poor, the EE issued (6 and 27 January
2010) show cause notices and due to non receipt of satisfactory reply from the
contractor, the contract was terminated (May 2010) under clause-3.
The contractor appealed (June 2010) to the SE for closure of contract under
clause-14 instead of clause-3 showing the justification of hindrance by Forest
Department. On the recommendation of the SE (July 2010) and CE (July
2010), the E-in-C, PWD submitted (September 2010) the proposal to the
Government, which was approved (October 2010) on the ground of forest land
dispute.

On further scrutiny of the correspondence with contractor and forest
department, we observed that the contention of contractor justifying the delay
due to hindrance of Forest Department was not correct and not based on facts
as corroborated from the EE’s letter (May 2010), wherein it was informed to
the SE that though the road is situated in forest area, but there was no
hindrances of forest as such. The EE, further, mentioned that the reasons put
forth by the contractor were totally incorrect and the basic reasons behind poor
progress were due to financial incapability, poor management and lack of will
power of the contractor.

While recommending the case for closure under clause-14 instead of clause-3,
the SE had also mentioned that the Forest department had not made any
correspondence objecting to the execution of the road work. He further stated
that the road was alse sanctioned for development under the Left Wing
Extremism scheme of Gol and the balance work was not required.

From the above it is evident that change in clause for foreclosure of contract
from clause-3 to clause-14 was not based on facts and this had resulted in
undue aid of = 47.02 lakh’ to the contractor as no penalty was recoverable
under clause-14. Further, the justification for non requirement of balance work
was also not correct as the balance work was awarded (March 2012) to another
contractor at 89 per cent above SOR and was paid (November 2012) an
amount of € 2.24 crore. The award of balance work at higher rate also resulted
in extra cost of € 1.46 crore”.

In reply, the EE, Ambikapur stated (June 2013) that no objection certificate
was received from the Forest Department (June 2010) after completion of the
agreement period (May 2010). The contractor had refused to continue the
work due to hindrance occurred by the Forest Department and requested to

2.4.8.6 Non-recovery of = 10.66 lakh towards difference of cost of bulk and
packed bitumen

As per orders of E-in-C, PWD (December 2004) road transportation of bulk
bitumen was allowed in place of packed bitumen for use in bituminous work
with the condition to recover at the rate of 1100 per MT on the consumption
of bitumen on road work, which were executed as per the SOR of November
2002.

Scrutiny of records pertaining to construction of Nandghat Bhatapara
Chandkhuri road under CRF scheme revealed that the contractors had used
969.112 MT of bulk bitumen in the road works. As per the orders issued by
Government, recovery of = 10.66 lakh (969,112 MT x & 1100) was to be made
from the contractor. However no action was taken by the EE, PWD (B&R)
Division, Balodabazar to recover the same from the contractor. This resulted
in extension of undue aid of = 10.66 lakh to the contractor and subsequent loss
to Government.

On this being pointed out in audit, the EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Balodabazar
stated (June 2013) that necessary action will be taken after scrutiny.

2.4.8.8 Failure of the Executive Engineer to encash the Bank Guarantees

As per special conditions of the contract, the contractor was responsible for
performance of work carried out by him for a period of 36 months from the

furnished by him at the rate of 15 per cent of the contract value. After two
years of completion of construction, 50 per cent of available performance

satisfaction of the EE. The balance amount of performance guarantee shall be
returned after three years of completion.

The contractor executed Dudhwa-Nagri-Basin road under CRF had submitted

Further, the contractor was paid हैं. 4.25 lakh for disposal of the excess
excavated earth at the rate of हैं 52.80 per cum (at the rate of हैं 52.80 x
8532.15 cum less 5.55 per cent below tender). Since the item no. 2.6 already
included the lead for disposal of earth, the additional payment made to the
contractor for disposal of excavated earth in the instant case resulted in excess
payment to the contractor.

In reply, the EE stated (June 2013) that item no. 2.6 includes all lifts and leads
only up to one km. Therefore, excavated earth transported beyond one km has
been paid separately. Further the excavated earth was not suitable for using in
embankment, hence selected soil was used.

The reply of the EE is factually incorrect as the item no.2.6 (SOR 2005),
included all lifts and leads without any limitation of distance. Payment of lead
beyond one km was separately provided in the revised SOR effective from
April 2009, which was not applicable to the present contract. Further, it was
also evident from the EE’s letter (June 2008) that the contractor disposed
8532.15 cum earth only and utilised the balance earth in the work for
construction of embankment instead of bringing selected soil from borrow
area.

(ii) Similarly scrutiny of the estimates and measurement books of the work
of widening of Durg city road under MNP revealed that the estimates were
prepared on the basis of SOR effective from April 2005. As per item no. 2.6 of
the SOR, an amount of = 42.84 lakh was paid to the contractor at the rate of
हैं [[] per cum for execution of 38604.14 cum of earthwork.

In addition to the above payment, an amount of हैं 24.41 lakh (at the rate of
% 61.20 per cum for 38604.14 cum) was also paid to the contractor for
disposal of excavated earth. Since the earlier payment as per item no. 2.6
included all leads, additional payment of % 24.41 lakh was inadmissible and
resulted in excess payment to the contractor.

In reply, the EE, Durg stated (May 2013) that SOR item no. 2.6 is provided
for restricting ponding of water. In case of excavated material needs to be
transported away from the site, the SOR item No.5.5-transportation of metal
by tipper for lead upto five km would be applicable.

The fact remains that the item no.2.6 of SOR included all leads without
limitation of any distance. Further, item no. 5.5 mentioned by the EE is not
applicable in the instant case as no transportation of metal is involved in the
work.

2.4.8.10 Execution of work without adhering to specifications

As per clause 507.3.3 and 509.3.3 of the specifications for Road and Bridge
works issued by MORT&H, execution of Semi-dense Bituminous Concrete
(SDBC) requires mix design duly approved by the Engineer-in-charge and
should be submitted by the contractor in writing at least 20 days before the
start of work.

Scrutiny of records pertaining to construction of Nandghat-Bhatapara-
Chandkhuri road sanctioned under CRF revealed that the item of SDBC
valuing % 1.32 crore (at the rate of ¥ 2860 x 4110.33 cum + 12 per cent) was

executed (2007-08) without approval of the mix design as required under

We further observed that as per inspection note (August 2007) of SE, the road
got damaged just after five months of its completion, which was within the
performance guarantee period of the contract. Consequently, the PWD
incurred expenditure of हैं 39.42 lakh (% 17.14 + हैं 22.28) for repair of the

audit (June 2013) as the matter was reported to be sub-judice. Thus execution

substandard work of € 1.32 crore.

On this being pointed out (June 2013) in audit, the EE, Balodabazar stated
