not be found on records.

The non availability of record relating to approved mix design and damage to
the road immediately after its construction indicate the possibility that
MORT&H specifications were not followed while executing the work.

2.4811 Doubtful execution of work valuing रैं 18.07 lakh in reserve forest
area without clearance from forest department

As per para 4.017 of WD Manual, the measurement book (MB) is a most
important record. It is the basis of all accounts of quantities of work done and
it must contain such a complete record of facts as to be conclusive evidence in
court of law, The description of work must be lucid to admit easy
identification. Every measurement, at the time it is taken, must be recorded
directly in the MB. The situation of work should be mentioned on the occasion
of each measurement.

We observed from the records of construction of Kawardha-Rengakhar road
under MNP that payment of हैं 18.07 lakh was made to the contractor for
excavation of embankment (16279 cum) between the stretch from km 17/10 to
18/6, which was under reserve forest area. Since the stretch from km 17/10 to
18/6 was under reserve forest area and no clearance for executing the work in
the forest area was also obtained from the Forest Department, the entries made
in MB and payment made on the basis of the measurement appears doubtful.

On this being pointed out, the EE stated (December 2013) that measurements
were recorded erroneously in absence of kilometre stone and no work was
executed between the said stretches due to reserve forest area. The EE also
stated that these measurements were corrected and necessary deduction will be
made in the final bill due for payment.

The fact remains that the measurements recorded were doubtful and also is
indicative of lack of due diligence on the part of EE.

2.4.8.12 Irregular and avoidable extra expenditure of % 20.19 lakh due to
application of tack coat over freshly laid bituminous surface

Clause 503.1 of MORT&H specifications stipulates that where the material to
receive an overlay was a freshly laid bituminous layer prior to laying SDBC
item, that has not been subjected to traffic or contaminated by dust, a tack coat
is not mandatory.

2.4.8.13 Irregular execution of work amounting to रैं 1.62 crore in violation
of Government’s instructions.

As per the instructions issued (July 1998 and April 1999) by E-in-C, PWD, no
supplementary work beyond the scope of the original work should be awarded.
In unavoidable circumstances the supplementary work was to be limited to 25
per cent of contract value of original work. The Government also reiterated
(June 2004) that the supplementary schedule can be sanctioned by EE up to 25
per cent with the written permission of SE. Any supplementary schedule
beyond 25 per cent is to be sanctioned after permission of the Government.

Further, as per clause 7.11 of the contract, the Engineer-in-charge can increase
or decrease in scope of work to the total limit of (+) 25 per cent of any item(s)
but up to (+) 10 per cent of the contract value only after obtaining the approval
of the authority competent to accord Technical Sanction.

Scrutiny of records of EE, PWD (B&R) Division, Ambikapur revealed that in
violation of above provisions, excess quantities of earthwork valuing
रू 1.62 crore were executed in two contracts without obtaining sanction from
Government. The increase in value of work was due to abnormal increase in
the item of earth work as detailed below:

The fact remaining that the extra work was executed without obtaining
sanction from the Government as required under the above provisions.

2.4.9 Conclusion

Due to inadequate planning and non prioritisation of road, there was
overlapping of sanctions. The PWD also failed to make detailed survey and
investigation before execution of road works which led to significant
variations in quantities and delay in completion of the works. Execution of
road works without availability of required land and forest clearance delayed
the works and increased the cost of the works. Failure of the PWD to adhere to
the Government’s instructions and contractual conditions led to unauthorised
diversion of Minimum Needs Programme funds, non recovery of penalty,
payment of inadmissible price escalation, irregular execution of works,
superfluous expenditure, excess payment etc.

2.4.10. Recommendation

e The Public Works Department should properly plan and finalise a list
of priority roads required to be developed under different schemes to
avoid overlapping of sanctions under different schemes.

e The works should be taken up afier detailed survey and investigation
and afier finalising the availability of land fiee from all encumbrances
to avoid delay in completion of works.

e Funds should be utilised in a time bound manner for the earmarked
projects.
e Specifications and provisions of the manual should be strictly adhered

to complete the works in an economic, efficient and effective manner.

ary
The Sports and Youth Welfare Department is responsible for promoting and
conducting sports activities in the State. The main activities of the Department
involve implementation of Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan
(PYKKA) (a Centrally Sponsored Scheme), training to players, organising
tural sports competition and women’s sports festival, financial assistance to
various sports organisations, encouragement and prizes for the talented
players, organising cultural programmes, youth activities and construction of
sports infrastructure in the State.

The Performance Audit of the functioning of the Department was conducted
covering the period from 2010-11 to 2012-13 to examine the economy,
efficiency and effectiveness of its significant activities. The major audit
findings are discussed below:

e Preparation of incomplete Detailed Project Report (DPR) for
implementation of Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan
(PYKKA) and delay in selection of implementing agency resulted in
blockage of = 5.05 crore for 18 months.

{Paragraph 2.5.6.2 (a) and 2.5.8.1}

e There were persistent savings ranging from 10 to 88 per cent during
2010-11 to 2012-13 due to non- utilisation of fund.

{Paragraph 2.5.7.1 (a)}

e = Irregular expenditure of हैं 1.84 crore was incurred on purchase of
multi gym and consumable items from PYKKA funds.

(Paragraph 2.5.8.2)

e@ The Department failed to submit the general organizational plan as
envisaged in the host city contract for 37" National Games and did
not make any tangible progress in preparation of hosting the games
even after lapse of three and half years.

(Paragraph 2.5.9)

e Ill planning and delay in issue of work order led to avoidable
escalation payment of € 4.39 crore.

{Paragraph 2.5.11 (a)}

112


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

1 Introduc

Sports is listed in the State list of the Seventh Schedule of the Constitution.
As such, it is the prime responsibility of the State Government to ensure
development of sports activities in the State. Government of India (Gol) plays
a supplementary role in the development of sports. Sport and Youth Welfare
Department (SY WD), Government of Chhattisgarh is the principal agency for
promoting and conducting sports activities in the State. The State Government
introduced Sports Policy 2001 to create sports culture in the State and
implement all the sports related schemes and programme at Gram Panchayat
level. The main activities of the Department involve providing training to
players, organising rural sports competition and women’s sports festival,
financial assistance to various sports organisations, encouragement of the
talented players, implementation of Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan
(PYKKA), organizing cultural programmes and youth activities and
construction of sports infrastructure in the State.

The performance of the State in terms of medal tally at National level
continues to be below average than the less populated and backward States
such as Manipur and Jammu and Kashmir. In the last two National Games
(2007 at Guwahati and 2011 at Ranchi), organised by the Indian Olympic
Association (IOA), the State was placed in 21" position with 06 and 07 medals
respectively. Even to two other newly formed States viz. Jharkhand and
Uttarakhand got 22 and 13 medals in 2007 and 96 and 13 medals in 2011
respectively.

The Chhattisgarh Olympic Association (COA) which was affiliated to the
Indian Olympic Association (IOC) in the year 2001 has been active in
spreading sports culture in the State. The Indian Olympic Association has
allotted hoisting of the 37" National Games scheduled to be held in the year
2017, to Chhattisgarh.

2.5.2 Organisational structure

The Principal Secretary, Government of Chhattisgarh, Sports & Youth
Welfare Department is the overall in-charge of the Department at Government
level and the Commissioner/Director is the administrative head of the
Department. Further, the Commissioner/Director is assisted by Joint Director,
Deputy Director, Assistant Director, Sports and Youth Welfare Officer and
other officials at Directorate level. The district office is headed by the District
Sports and Youth Welfare Officer who is assisted by Senior Sports
Officers/Sports Officers, Senior Coach/ trainers and other officers.

2.5.3 Audit objectives
The Audit objectives were to evaluate whether:
© Planning for sports development was proper and adequate.

© Financial Management was efficient and effective.

© Implementation of various schemes and programme management by
the department was efficient, effective and economical.

Store Management was efficient and effective.
Human Resources Management was efficient and effective.

Internal Control Mechanism was in place and monitoring was
effective.

2.5

5 Audit scope and methodology

In order to conduct the Performance Audit of the functioning of the
Department of Sports and Youth Welfare Department (SYWD), apart from
Directorate, Sports and Youth Welfare, 10 out of 27 districts were selected
for audit. The period of coverage was from 2010-11 to 2012-13. The audit was
conducted from May to July 2013. Various aspects including planning,
financial management, programme management, human resource management
and intemal controls were covered in the Performance Audit. An entry
conference was held (10" June 2013) with the Principal Secretary, SYWD,
Chhattisgarh Government wherein the audit methodology, objectives and
coverage were discussed. The exit conference was held (17" January 2014)
with the Principal Secretary, SYWD and the views of the departmental
authorities were incorporated in the relevant observations.

2.5.6 Institutional weaknesses

In order to ensure efficient and effective implementation of schemes,
Department was required to adhere to the existing sports policy, prepare
Perspective Plan and Annual Action Plan and proper monitoring of schemes at
State, District, Block and Village level. During the Performance Audit,
institutional weaknesses such as non adherence to Sports Policy 2001,
preparation of incomplete DPR for implementation of PYKKA, provision of
inadequate funds and non creation of Chhattisgarh Sports Council etc were
noticed as discussed in the succeeding paragraphs:

2.5.6.1 Non-adherence to Sports Policy 2001
Identification and development of Sports Playground

Sports Policy 2001 of the State inter-alia includes conducting survey of
available sports playgrounds and open fields which are suitable for playground
in the State. The Policy further provides that necessary steps are also to be
taken for marking and security of all existing playgrounds. Similarly,
playground of popular game should be developed and after creating a rural
sports centre, regular practice should be started at village level. Playground at
higher secondary school at block level, sports complex at district leyel and
sports training centre at State level should be constructed for development of
sports activities in the State.

The Government had undertaken initiatives for construction of eight sports
infrastructure facilities ie. Sports Complex, Hockey Astroturf, Stadiums and
Athletic track ete in different districts headquarters and International Cricket
Stadium at Raipur.

However, Scrutiny of records revealed that the Department had not conducted
any survey on availability of sports facilities, sports play grounds and open
fields suitable for playground and marking of sports playground in the State
even after lapse of more than 12 years since the introduction of Sports Policy
The Department had also not taken any initiative to construct playground of
popular games such as Athletics, Volleyball, Kabbadi, Football and Kho-Kho
at village level or any playground at higher secondary school at block level.

29.10.2011

Picture showing sports competitions for women under PYKKA were org: in open field at Purai

Information furnished by the test checked districts also revealed that data
relating to the availability, non-availability जात constructed/ongoing
playground from other sources ie. Mahatma Gandhi National Rural
Employment Guarantee Act (MNREGA), ete at village level and school level
were not available with the Department. Information collected from all the
districts revealed that in 12 out of 27 districts there was no stadium, mini
stadium and indoor stadium as discussed in para no 2.5.10 (a).

From the above it is clear that due to non adherence to the Sports Policy,
objective of the Department such as developing sports culture in the State,
implementation of all sports related schemes and programmes at District,
Block and Village level were not achieved in last 12 years.

During the exit conference the Government stated (January 2014) that the

Rajnandgaon, Bilaspur etc) is in completion stage. Government further added
that for development of Sports infrastructure at block level, 25 places were
identified and works sanctioned under MGNREGA and for promoting sports
culture in the State, a new scheme Yuva Bharat Darshan is being launched
from this year. It was also assured that detailed survey of available
playground, marking and security of all playgrounds and other aspects as
envisaged in the sports policy will be made.

2.5.6.2 Planning

With a view to ensure sports development as integral aspects of youth
development and youth development as critical to accelerated National
development, the National Sports Policy 2001 lays special emphasis on
“Broad-basing of Sports” through grass root level sports activity. On the basis
of National Sports Policy, State Sports Policy had also been formulated in the
year 2001. The Gol launched the PYKKA in May 2008 aiming to achieve the
above objectives by providing basic sports infrastructure and equipment at the
Panchayat level and encouraging sports and games in rural areas through
annual competitions at the Block, District, State and National level. Scrutiny
of records related to planning revealed deficiencies such as, preparation of
incomplete Detailed Project Report (DPRs) for implementation of PYKKA,
non creation of Chhattisgarh Sports Council etc. which are discussed below:

(a) Preparation of incomplete DPR for implementation of PYKKA

As per the PYKKA guidelines issued by Ministry of Youth Affairs and Sports
(MoYAS), Gol, the programme will cover all village and block panchayats in
the country. The duration of the programme would be 10 years beginning from
2007-08 and will be implemented in a phased manner over the Eleventh
(2007-08 to 2011-12) and Twelfth plan periods (2012-13 to 2016-17). The
guidelines further provide for preparation of DPR with Perspective Plan and
Annual Action Plan by the States and while making these plans, the following
aspects also need special consideration.

e The Panchayats that are able to mobilise/tie-up matching amount from
own resources, State Government contribution and schemes such as
Member of Parliament Local Area Development (MPLAD), Member
of Legislative Assembly Local Area Development (MLALAD),
Backward Region Grant Fund (BRGF) ete and Panchayat that already
have basic sports infrastructure would get priority in terms of
coverage.

Scrutiny of records revealed that though the SYWD had prepared (January
2011) DPR with Perspective Plan and Annual Action Plan for implementation
of PYKKA scheme but had not incorporated any information on the efforts
made by the Panchayats to mobilise/tie-up matching amount through their own
resources or from other sources as mentioned above. It was further observed
that the SYWD had issued instruction (November 2010) to the District offices
to furnish detailed information of sports activities, availability of playground

116


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

and sports infrastructure in village, block and school level, availability of
coach in the village etc, so that the same could be incorporated in the DPRs.
But, the required information was not furnished to the SYWD before
preparation of DPRs. In the test checked districts, it was observed that the
detailed information was submitted by the District Sports and Youth Welfare
(DSYW) Officer to the SYWD with delay ranging from five to 22 months. In
absence of this, the Department selected (January 2011) 10 to 12 per cent of
villages/blocks for every year in each district in the DPRs for implementation
of the scheme without having proper justification. The DPR was accepted by
the MoYAS, Gol in November 2011. As per this, the PYKKA scheme was
being implemented.

On this being pointed out the Director, SYWD stated (August 2013) that DPR
for implementation of PYKKA was prepared with the help of Education and
Tribal Welfare Departments. It was further stated that for selecting the Village
under PYKKA, help of Sports Officials of PYKKA_ centre/school
principal/Head Masters/Secretary of the Panchayat were taken. During the exit
conference the Government stated (January 2014) that all these aspects will be
taken into account while preparing DPR.

The fact remains that DPR did not incorporate aspects as prescribed in the
guidelines. Further, informations stated to have been received from Sports
Official of PYKKA centre/schools on the basis of which the DPR was
prepared, were not produced to audit.

(b) Non-creation of Chhattisgarh Sports Council

As per the Sports Policy 2001, to develop sports activities in the State,
Chhattisgarh Sports Council, a committee of reputed Sports
persons/journalists and other reputed personalities, was to be formed for
development of sports and the council will advise the State Government.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the Chhattisgarh Sports Council was not
formed even afier lapse of 12 years of formation of the State. The non
formation of the Council may be one of the reasons for inadequate
development of infrastructure and non implementation of various schemes
effectively as discussed in the subsequent paragraph.

During the exit conference the Government stated (January 2014) that prior to
the year 2000, there was a Sport Council in Madhya Pradesh. Accordingly
same was provided in the Sports Policy 2001. However, as the SYWD is
working for the development of Sports Organisation in the State would
provide necessary advice on sports activities to the State Government, Sports
Council is not required. He added that no allocation in budget will be made for
Chhattisgarh Sports Council in future

The fact remains that the Sports Policy of the State clearly provides for
existence of Sports Council in the State and same was not formed despite
provision of funds in the budget.

2.5.7.2 Non-submission of utilisation certificate

As per the Financial Assistance Rule 2007 (General 14), every sports
organisation which receives financial assistance for sports activities such as
organising State/National competition, training to sports persons and travelling
expenses of sports person ftom the SYWD should submit the utilisation
certificate (UCs) within two months of sanction of assistance to the DSYW
office.

During scrutiny of the records of the test checked dis it was observed
that 43 sports organisations were paid financial assistance aggregating to
रें 251 crore ranging between % 5,000 to हैं 20 lakh in 191 occasions for
organising sports activities during 2009-10 to 2012-13. Out of these, only in
21 cases the UCs were submitted within the stipulated period by the Sports
organizations. While in 92 cases, delays in submission of UCs ranged between
one to 15 months. The position in the remaining 78 cases could not be
ascertained due to non furnishing of date of submission of UCs by the DSYW
officer to Audit.

In reply, the DSYW Officers of the concerned districts stated that the sports
organisations submitted the utilisation certificate along with audit certificate
issued by the Chartered Accountant, hence there was delay in submission of
UCs. During the exit conference the Government stated (January 2014) that
instruction will be issued to the Sports Organisation to ensure submission of
the UCs within prescribed time.

Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA)

Gol launched (May 2008) Panchayat Yuva Krida aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA)

Audit Report on General, Social and Economic (Non-PSUs) Sectors for the year ended 31 March 2013

a Centrally Sponsored Scheme to encourage and promote sports and games
among rural youth by providing them access to basic sports infrastructure and
equipment at the panchayat level and opportunity to participate in sports
competitions at the block and district levels leading to further opportunities for
talented sports persons emerging from this process to receive advanced
training and exposure and participate and excel in State, National and
International Tournaments. Although the scheme launched in the year 2007-08
throughout the country, it was implemented in the State in the year 2009-10.
Under the scheme, all the Village & Block Panchayat of the State are to be
covered within a period of 10 years ending 2017. The components of PYKKA
are shown in Appendix 2.5.3.

Scrutiny of records revealed delay in implementation of the scheme, irregular
purchases, non-appointment of required number of Kridashree’ in the villages
and non payment of honorarium to Kridashree, coverage of village and block
panchayats, less participation of village panchayats in the block level
competition and non utilization of funds under PYKKA as discussed in the
succeeding paragraphs.

2.5.8.1 Delay in implementation of scheme

As per para 12.4 of operational guidelines for implementation of PYKKA
issued (May 2009) by MoYAS, Gol, the designated agency at the State level ,
to which funds should be released, would be responsible for utilisation of
funds appropriately as per terms and conditions prescribed in the sanction
letter. Further, as per the sanction letter issued (June 2009) by Gol, the grantee
shall submit the utilisation certificate in the prescribed format within twelve
months of the close of financial year with the audited statement of accounts.
The above guideline also provides that the PYKKA should be implemented in
a phased manner so as to cover all the villages by 2016-17.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the Gol released the first installment of
% 3.94 crore as one time capital grant for the development of sports
infrastructure, हैं 50.50 lakh as annual acquisition grant for procurement of
sports equipment and हैं 60.50 lakh as operational grant for meeting operational
expenses of non-competitive activities including honorarium to Kridashree,
maintenance of infrastructure etc in June 2009 for implementation of PYKKA
to the State Government. In October 2009, the State level PYKKA Samiti was
formed for implementation of the scheme. The State Government decided
(March 2010) to utilise the one time capital grant and annual acquisition grant
through the Zilla Panchayat (ZP), Janpad Panchayat (JP) and village
panchayat and Annual Operation grant through DSYW Office. For preparing
DPR, the Department initiated steps by collecting information from
districts/blocks level official in November 2010 (as discussed in the para
2.5.6.2 (a)) and in January 2011, the DPR was sent to Gol. The DPR was
approved by Gol in November 2011. The Directorate, SYWD allotted the
funds to the Chief Executive Officer (CEOs) ZPs of all Districts in January
2011. During February 2011 to June 2012, the funds were utilized by the ZPs

From the above, it may be observed that the SYWD had finalised the

order for release of funds to the CEO, ZP in January 2011 and there was delay
in submission of UCs by the ZP. Thus, there was delay in implementation of
scheme.

Reply confirms the audit observation. Though the fund was sanctioned in the
year 2009-10, the State Government could not utilise the funds in absence of
adequate planning for implementation of the scheme.

one lakh is provided to each village panchayat having minimum 4600
population with additional funding on prorata basis for additional population
size beyond the National average. Similarly रैं five lakh was provided to each
block panchayat selected in the year (2011-12) on the basis of DPR. Further as
per para 9.1 of the guidelines, the one time capital grant could be utilised for
permanent nature of work such as leveling of land, development of playfield,
athletics track, goal posts, poles for net games, fencing of playfield,
development of water bodies, outdoor fitness equipment, seating structures,
Improvement of existing sports infrastructure, washroom/change room, indoor
facilities and other items permissible under the detailed State Action Plan.

Scrutiny of records revealed that instead of development of infrastructure the
State level PYKKA Samiti decided (October 2009) to utilise the one time
capital grant for purchase of multi gym and installed them in villages and
block panchayats, those having availability of room and had given their
consent to run the gym. As per this decision, the CEO, ZP purchased multi
gyms valuing = 118.16 lakh and installed at village and block level PYKKA

2.5.8.3 Appointment and functioning of Kridashree

As per para 4.9 of the PYKKA guidelines, selection of Kridashree would be
done by the implementing agency for maintaining and upgrading playfield,
motivating the players to attend regular sports activities, ensuring sports
equipments etc at the Panchayat level. They would also manage the sports
facilities and also act as a general trainer. All Kridashree will be trained
through an orientation course organized by the State Government with
technical support from the Sports Authority of India. The Kridashree is
eligible for honorarium of % 500 per month at the village level and % 1,000 at
the block level and the payment is to be made from Annual Operational Grant
provided under the schemes.

Scrutiny of records revealed following deficiencies relating to Kridashree:
(a) Non-appointment of required number of Kridashree

As per the DPR for implementation of PYKKA scheme, 2673 number of
Kridashree were to be appointed in the selected PYKKA centres of the village

123


Audit Report on General, Social and Economic (Non-PSUs) Sectors for the year ended 31 March 2013

and block during 2009-10 to 2012-13 in the test checked districts. The year
wise appointment of Kridashree in the test checked districts are given in
Appendix 2.5.4.

It may be observed that only 1082 number of Kridashree (41 per cent) were
appointed against the required number of 2673 and no Kridashree were
appointed in 1591 PYKKA centre. It was further observed that the percentage
of appointed kridashree ranged between 19 to 58 in the test checked districts.

On this being pointed out, the DSYW Officer of concerned districts stated
(July 2013) that appointment of Kridashree for the year 2009-10 and 2010-11
was approved by the State Level PYKKA samiti and for the year 2011-12 and
2012-13, no further approval for appointment was accorded by the samiti.

The fact remains that due to appointment of insufficient number of
Kridashree, maintaining and upgrading playfield and motivating the players as
envisaged in the guidelines could not be ensured.

(b) Non imparting of training to all Kridashree

As per the PYKKA guidelines, all Kridashree would be trained through an
orientation course organised by the State Government with technical support
from the Sports Authority of India.

Scrutiny of records of test checked districts revealed that out of
1082 Kridashree appointed in the PYKKA centre during the period 2009-13,
only 731 number of Kridashree were imparted above training. It was further
observed that while the required training was imparted to all Kridashree in
Dhamtari district, 38 to 82 per cent of Kridashree were imparted training in
the remaining districts.

On this being pointed out, the DSOs of selected districts stated (July 2013)
that the training camp was orgainised at Directorate level and due to non
organizing of training camp after 2010, the training was not imparted to
kridashree. The Director, SYWD stated (August 2013) that due to non-release
of funds from Gol, required training was not imparted to the Kridashree.

The fact remains that funds were not released by Gol due to delay in
finalization of DPR, delay in appointment of implementing agency and delay
in submission of UCs.

(c) Non payment of honorarium to Kridashree

As per the PYKKA guidelines, the Kridashree were eligible for monthly
honorarium of % 500 per month at the village level and हैं 1,000 at the block
level and the payment was to be made from Annual Operational Grant under
the PYKKA scheme.

Scrutiny of records revealed that the SYWD failed to get the funds
(operational grant) from Gol due to non utilisation of grant of PYKKA in time
and delay in submission of UCs as mentioned in the para no 2.5.8.1.
Consequently it was observed that the honorarium amounting to हैं 1.64 crore
was not paid to the Kridashree for period ranging from one to 43 months in
the test checked districts. The district wise details of number of kridushree to

2.5.8.4 Shortfall in coverage of village & block panchayat

As per para 5.4 of PYKKA Guidelines, 10 per cent of the Village & Block
panchayat were to be covered within a period of four years from 2008-09 to
2011-12 and 12 per cent were to be covered for the period of six years from
2012-13 to 2016-17 so that by the year 2016-17, 100 per cent coverage is to be
achieved in the State. Accordingly, DPR for implementation of the scheme
was finalized by the State and subsequently approved by the Gol.

As per the information furnished by SYWD during the period, as against the
target of 5106 village panchayat, only 673 village panchayats (13 per cent)
were covered and 4433 (86.81 percent) of village panchayats were not
covered even after five years of implementation of the scheme. Similarly
80.28 per cent of block panchayats were not covered during the five years of
implementation of the scheme. The less coverage of village and blocks was
mainly due to delay in implementation of PYKKA scheme despite release of
funds by Gol as discussed in para 2.5.8.1.

Thus, inadequate coverage of the villages and blocks in implementation of the
PYKKA scheme in the State led to inadequate promotion of sports and games
among rural youth. Further, due to slow progress, covering of all the villages
within 10 years as prescribed in the guidelines would not be possible.

In reply, the Director, SYWD stated (January 2014) that in the State, the
scheme was not implemented in the year 2008-09 and during the period only
fifty per cent grant of the year 2009-10 was received in the month of
June 2009 and the remaining fifty per cent and grant of 2010-11 and
2011-12 was received in the month of September 2012 only. During the exit
conference the Government stated (January 2014) that coverage of village and
block panchayat under PYKKA would be increased in future.

2.5.8.5 Less participation of village panchayat in the block level competition

As per para 8.1.4 of PYKKA guideline, Annual Competition grant is provided
for organizing PYKKA competition at the block and the district level. This
will be implemented through State Government/Union Territories with
technical support from the SAI. The competition will be organised at the
block, district, State and National level to complete the chain of competition as
pet the ongoing rural sports programme every year.

During scrutiny of the utilisation and progress report of the competition grant
of the 10 test checked districts, it was observed that the participation of village
panchayat was inadequate and was ranging from 6 per cent to 44 per cent
during the last three years (Appendix 2.5.6). Due to less participation of

125


Audit Report on General, Social and Economic (Non-PSUs) Sectors for the year ended 31 March 2013

village panchayat in the block level competition, the objective of
implementation of PYKKA could not be achieved.

In teply, the DSYW, of two districts’ replied (July 2013) that the less
participation was due to organising of PYKKA competition along with school
competition. The DSYW, of four districts” stated that due to insufficiency of
funds participation of all the villages was not possible. The DSYW, Raigarh
stated that 10 per cent Village Panchayat were identified for implementation
of PYKKA in the year 2009-10 and thereafter, 10 per cent of villages each in
every year were identified for implementation of PYKKA and the same
would increase the percentage of participation of village in future. In
remaining three districts no specific comments were given.

Thus due to less participation of villages in PYKKA competition at Block
level there was shortfall in achievement of the main objective of PYKKA to
encourage and promote sports and games among rural youth at panchayat
level.

2.5.8.6 Non-utilisation of PYKKA fund by the implementing agency

As per the PYKKA guidelines, the designated agency (SYWD) at the State
level will be responsible for utilisation of funds appropriately as per terms and
conditions of the guidelines.

Scrutiny of records revealed that out of = 5.26 crore released (January 2011) as
one time capital grant and % 50.50 lakh as annual acquisition grant for
implementation of PYKKA in the state. The implementing agencies i.e. CEO,
ZP, submitted UCs for %5.14 crore and % 49.95 lakh respectively to the
Directorate, SYWD. However, balance amount of % 12.79 lakh was not
refunded by the CEOs, ZP of 14 districts even after lapse of two years from
the date of release of funds to the agencies. Further, it was observed that the
Department had not taken any initiatives to obtain the UCs from the ZPs.

On this being pointed out, the Director, SYWD accepted the fact and stated
(July 2013) that information related to the expenditure of above amount was
not received from the CEO, ZPs. During the exit conference the Government
stated (January 2014) that necessary instruction would be issued to obtain the
UCs from implementing agencies.

The above replies confirm absence of mechanism for monitoring the
utilization of funds and submission of UCs.

2.5.9 Preparedness for National Games

The National Games events, which are held biannually under the guidance of
Indian Olympic Association (IOA), is being organized with the objective of
creating awareness among the States/Union Territories about the necessity for
creating sports infrastructure of international specifications and to raise the
standards of Indian Sports. The Indian Olympic Association has allotted
(March 2010) the 377 National Games to Chhattisgarh. The Host City

Bemetara and Durg

5 Bastar,Bilaspur, Kondagaon and Kanker

Chapter 2:Performauce Audit

Contract for organizing the 37" National Games was signed by the Indian
Olympic Association, Chhattisgarh Government and Chhattisgarh Olympic
Association on March 2010 at New Delhi.

The Host City Contract’s Basic Principles inter-alia provides that the
Government and State Olympic Association should create a National Game
Organizing Committee (NGOC) in a form ensuring it the status of a legal
person within six months from the contract. The principles further provide that
as soon as possible, but in any event not later than six months afier the
creation of the NGOC, it shall submit its general organization plan and plan of
organising the Games to IOA for its approval and a half yearly progress report
on the preparation of the Games, commencing two years before the Opening
Ceremony of the Games should be forwarded quarterly.

Scrutiny of records revealed that in July 2010, the Secretary, SYWD,
Government of Chhattisgarh designated as Chief Executive Officer of
National Games Secretariat and in August 2010, as per Chhattisgarh Society
Registration Act, 1973, Chhattisgarh National Games Secretariat was
constituted. Further, Governing Body of 34 members headed by the Chief
Minister, Chhattisgarh and Executive Committee of 19 members headed by
the Chief Secretary, Chhattisgarh Government were constituted to execute the
works for National Games Secretariat. However, the general organizational
plan of National Games Secretariat and of the Games was uot prepared by the
body for its approval by IOA. The half yearly and quarterly progress report on
preparation of National Games as mentioned above were also not sent to LOA
during last three and half years.

Details of meetings held for organizing National Games by the Governing
Body and Executive Committee are given in Appendix 2.5.7, from which it
may be observed that

and tendering procedure for DPR are being carried out. Necessary meetings of
Governing Body and Executive Committee would also be called very soon.

The fact remains that the State Government should have taken concrete steps
for preparedness of National Games which it had not initiated so far even after
lapse of three and half years.

2.5.10 Infrastructure

The major constraint in taking sports activity to the grassroots level is the
availability of limited basic sports infrastructure/ facilities in the country.
Further, the existing base is highly skewed, as it is largely concentrated in
urban areas, which account for not more than 25 per cent of the population.
The remaining 75 per cent of the population, which largely lives in rural areas,
are deprived of even rudimentary sporting facilities®.

Sports and Youth Welfare Department, Government of Chhattisgarh, had

examine whether the work were got executed by the contractor as per drawing
and design provided by the architect with due quality and also to examine the
bill submitted by the contractor for payment. Government Engineering
College (GEC), Raipur was appointed (November 2003) as the PMC for this
work. In between March 2003 to June 2004, Government had also initiated
efforts for raising loan from HUDCO for construction of this Stadium. In July

2004, the Government however decided to bear all expenditure of construction

Scrutiny of records revealed the followings:

(a) Excess expenditure of % 4.39 crore on escalation due to change in
design of foundation/structure of the stadium

As per clause 13 of the Special Condition of the Contract, the contractor was
entitled for payment of escalation for increase in prices of cement, material,
steel and labour.

Scrutiny of records revealed that though the contract for construction of the
stadium with the contractor was made during March 2003, work order was
issued to the contractor in September 2004 i.e. after 17 months of acceptance
of the tender. It was observed that approval of layout of the stadium and
permission for construction of stadium was taken from Capital Area
Development Authority (CADA) during November 2004. The finalisation of
foundation/structural design for construction of the Stadium was also delayed
till March 2005 due to refusal of earlier appointed PMC (GEC) and awarding
PMC work to another PMC (Stadium Nirman Samiti). Thus, at the initial
stage, progress of work was affected due to ill planning by the Government
and the stipulated period for completion of the work was extended twice ie.
initially up to December 2007 and then up to 31 October 2009. The work was
to be completed by September 2006 but due to slow progress of work, the
construction work was continued for 79 months instead of 24 months.

As this was an item rate tender and the contractor had quoted the rate for each
item on the basis of price index of March 2003, prices of all items including
material, labour, steel and cement increased by 41 to 181 per cent during the
period from March 2003 to October 2009. As per the clause 13, the contractor
was entitled to claim escalation payment due to increase in prices. The
contractor claimed (January 2005 to March 2009) escalation payment of
% 23.41 crore against admissible payment of हैं. 18.49 crore (upto April 2007)
through each running bill between January 2005 to March 2009 and the same
was approved by the Stadium Nirman Samiti justifying that slow progress of
work at the initial stages was not attributed to the contractor. Out of

130


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

% 23.41 crore, up to date escalation payment amounting to % 22.88 crore was
paid to the contractor as of September 2012. The department floated the
tender without finalising the funding arrangement and approval of drawing
and design etc.,which resulted in delay in completion of work and contractor
was paid escalation amounting to % 4.39 crore for the extended period i.e. May
2007 to October 2009.

Thus, finalisation of tender without funding arrangement, delay in issue of
work order and delay in approval of the layout, structural drawing and design
etc not only led to delay in completion of the work but also resulted in
avoidable payment of escalation of € 4.39 crore.

On this being pointed out, the Director, SYWD, stated (July 2013) that due to
delay in funds arrangement, approval of plan lay out from CADA, non
monitoring of construction work by the PMC and increase in price index of
materials, the above escalation payment was made.

Reply only confirms the audit observation that tender was floated before
approval of drawing and design and without finalising the funding
arrangement.

(b) Extension of undue aid to contractor

As per clause 3.23.1 of the Chhattisgarh PWD manual, Mobilisation advance
not exceeding five per cent of the contract amount and limited to = 10 lakh
shall be given if requested by contractor in writing within three months of the
date of order to commence the work. In such a case, a Bank Guarantee for the
amount shall be furnished by the contractor before sanction of the advance.
This advance shall bear interest at the rate of 14 per cent per annum.

During scrutiny of records, it was observed that in disregard to the provision
of PWD manual, mobilisation advance to the extent of 15 per cent of the
accepted value of tender without bearing any interest was provided in the
clause 37 of agreement of this work. As per the agreement, mobilisation
advance aggregating to 8.81 crore was paid to the contractor between March
2003 to January 2005 in three installments ie. €5.00 crore in March 2003,
र 2.50 crore in October 2004 and % 1.31 crore in January 2005. Advance
amounting to रैं 8.81 crore was recovered from the interim payment certificates
submitted by the contractor during January 2005 to October 2008.
As mentioned in the preceding paragraph, the Department had taken almost
two years for preparation of structural drawing and design and approval of
layout, from CADA. During this period, the contractor retained the
mobilisation advance of % 5.00 crore with him without executing any work.

Thus, sanction of interest free mobilisation advance to the contractor in
violation of the manual provision was irregular and led to undue financial
benefit of € 4.73 crore to the contractor.

In reply, the Director, SYWD stated (July 2013) that as per clause 37 of the
agreement, mobilisation advance was paid to the contractor.

’  %8.81 crore *14/100=1.23*46(January 2005 to October 2008)/12-% 4.73 crore

The agreement was not drawn in accordance with the provisions prescribed in
the PWD manual. Further, the work also could not be completed in time even
after sanction of the advance.

(c) Excess payment to Government Engineering College for

As per the general condition of contract, a PMC was to be appointed by the

र₹ 27.03 lakh were allotted during the period 2010-11 to 2012-13 under the
scheme. However, only 157 talented boys and girls in sports were identified in
three districts. Information regarding their admission into the training centre of
SAI/Sports Complex maintained by the Tribal Department was not furnished

under the National Talent Search, list were not furnished to the audit, [n the

2.5.12.2 Organising of adventure sports without formulation of guidelines

As per the Sports Policy, Adventure Sports was to be organised for developing
confidence among the youth in handling risk and for overall development of
the youth alongwith adventure. For organising event every year, SYWD will
prepare a calendar. Further as per the guideline for adventure sports issued by
the Ministry of Tourism (MoT), Gol, the Adventure Tour Operators (ATO)
have to apply for recognition to operate as ATO anywhere in the country. As
per the availability of the equipment and safety measure, they are issued ‘A’,
“B’ and ‘C” categories licence which could be reviewed by the accreditation
committee within six months.

Duting scrutiny of records and information provided by the Department, it was
observed that the department had organised 11 adventure sports event!’ in 10
districts’? of the State during the period 2010-11 to 2012-13 and incurred
expenditure of हैं 64.78 lakh for the purpose. It was further observed that the
department conducted the Adventure sports by shortlisting event manager
through quotation, without ascertaining their recognition as ATO by the MoT,
Gol. It was also observed that department had not executed any agreement
with the event manager for organising adventure sports. Due to non adherence
of MoT guidelines and in absence of agreement, eligibility of event manager
to organizing adventure sports and safety measures taken during such
adventurous event could not be ascertained by audit.

On this being pointed out, the Director, SYWD stated (August 2013) that at

present, there is no guidelines for conducting adventure games. However,
necessary guidelines would be prepared in future.

Reply is not acceptable as the guidelines for organizing adventure sports
issued by MoT, Gol was not adhered to while conducting the above game.

13 Store Management

A sound system of store management involves efficient planning of purchase
requirements, economic procurement, control over issues, timely accounting
and safe physical custody. Scrutiny of records revealed the followings:

(a) Physical verification of stores

Rule 133 of the Chhattisgarh Financial Code provides for physical verification
of all stores once in a year as prescribed by respective Heads of Departments.
A certificate of verification of stores with its results should be recorded on the
list, inventory or account, as the case may be, where such verification is
carried out.

Scrutiny of the records and information furnished by the Department revealed
that no physical verification of stores during the period 2010-11 to 2012-13
was conducted at Directorate office and DSYW office, Kanker. Though the
DSYW of nine test checked districts reported that physical verification was
done but no certificate in support of the same was provided to audit. Tt was
further observed that due to non-availability of space for keeping the sports
equipments in the office of the DSO, Bastar these equipments were kept in the
garage of Police Rakshit Kendra, Bastar as shown in the photograph.

Picture showing Stock kept in garage at Police Rakshit Kendra, Bastar

The Director, SYWD replied that after conducting the physical verification of
the stores and stock, the same will be shown to audit. The DSYW, Kanker
stated that physical verification was net done. During the exit conference the
Government stated (January 2014) that certification and physical verification
would be ensured.

The above replies confirm that no physical verification of stores was done in
the Directorate and DSYW, Kanker office.

(b) Procurement without immediate requirement

As per Rule 118 of the Chhattisgarh Financial Code Volume-I, purchases
should be made only after assessment of definite requirements. Further
Chhattisgarh State Treasury Code Rule 284 (1) also states that no money

134


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

should be drawn from the treasury until it is needed for immediate
disbursement. Withdrawal of money in anticipation of expenditure or to avoid
budget lapses is a serious irregularity

Scrutiny of records of the Directorate revealed that the Director, SYWD had
purchased (February 2013) five sets of kabaddi mat valuing % 34.78 lakh from
Eskay Sports Patiala as per the requisition of the Kabaddi Association. The
supply of the above purchase was made by the supplier in the month of
February 2013 and the same was entered in the stock register. It was however
observed that the above stock was not issued to the association even after
lapse of six months and the stock was lying in the store of the Directorate.
Thus, procurement of sports items without immediate requirement resulted in
blocking of funds of = 34.78 lakh.

The Director, SYWD, stated (August 2013) that information regarding lying
of mats and its possession have been called for from the State Level Kabaddi
Association. After obtaining the information, the mats would be distributed.
During the exit conference the Government stated (January 2014) that
equipments as and when purchased would be issued to the Sports Organisation
concerned immediately.

With a view to empower the youth against economic, social and cultural

challenges and in order to perform a qualitative analysis of all the policies
designed for them, a Youth Commission, was formed (2005-06) by the
SYWD. The main objective of the Youth Commission is to communicate
latest information in the field of development of education, commerce,
science, art and culture, entertainment, adventure sports, tourism ete at
National and International level to the young generation of the State.

During test check of the records of the DSO, Raipur, it was observed that an
amount of हैं 1.60 crore'’ was allotted for assistance to Youth Commission
during last three years. Out of this, an amount of % 66.58 lakh (42 per cent)
was spent on salaries, travelling expenses, house rent, diesel expenses, vehicle,
mobile and repairing, which were no way concerned with the development of
youth activities and remaining 58 per cent of allocated funds were utilised in
conducting Play, Group dances, folk dances etc. Thus, a large percentage of
funds allotted for development of youth were utilised for office expenses.

On this being pointed out the Director, SYWD stated (August 2013) that from
July 2012 to March 2013, various youth activities were being organised in
four districts. However, no specific comment was offered regarding above
expenditure incurred for office expenses.

Considering the huge proportion of allotment spent on salaries and other
expenses, Government may consider fixing norms for such expenses.

2.

6 Monitor:

ig

Monitoring activities

Systematic, result-oriented and intensified field monitoring are essential to
assess the impact and implementation of the schemes as per guidelines. Some
of the observations indicating lack of monitoring by the Department are
discussed below:

Internal Controls
(a) Internal audit

Internal audit is a vital component of the internal control mechanism. Scrutiny
of records revealed that though there was an internal audit wing at the
Directorate, no internal audit was conducted in the test checked offices during
the period from 2010-11 to 2012-13.

On this being pointed out, it was stated (August 2013) that due to shortage of
staff, internal audit was not conducted.

The reply is not acceptable as no internal audit has been conducted by the
Department despite posting of two auditors.

(b) Inspection by higher authorities

Periodic inspection by the departmental officers is an important and effective
tool with the management to ensure compliance of rules, procedures and
guidelines for proper implementation of the schemes. It was observed that the
higher authorities viz. Director, Deputy Director and Assistant Director had.
not inspected any of the test checked districts during the last three years.

136


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

During the exit conference the Government stated (January 2014) that the
DSYW offices were under the supervision of Police Department as the
drawing and disbursing power was vested with them. From this year, the
drawing and disbursing power was allotted to DSYW offices. Thus, necessary
steps would be taken to conduct internal audit in future.

17 Conclusion

The Sports and Youth Welfare Department is responsible for promoting and
conducting sports activities in the State. We noticed deficiencies in planning
such as non adherence to Sports Policy 2001, preparation of incomplete
Detailed Project Report (DPR) for implementation of Panchayat Yuva Krida
aur Khel Abhiyan (PYKKA) etc. Further, irregularities such as non-utilisation
of funds, irregular purchases, non-appointment of required number of
Kridashree, shorttall in coverage of village and block panchayat etc. were also
noticed in implementation of PYKKA. Though the Indian Olympic
Association (IOC) allotted (2010) to State hosting of 37" National Games in
2017, no concrete steps had been initiated by the State Government for
creation of necessary infrastructure during last three and half years. We also
observed irregularities such as excess expenditure on escalation due to change
in design and scope of work, undue aid to contractor on mobilisation advance,
etc. Shortage of manpower at various levels in the Department resulted in
ineffective implementation and monitoring of the activities. Internal control
which includes internal audit and inspection were not effective.

18 Recommendations

We recommend that the Department should:

@ Implementation of Panchayat Youth Krida aur Khel Abhiyan scheme
as per Gol guidelines should he ensured to create sports infrastructure
and promote sports activities in the State.

Utilisation of funds as provided in the budget should be ensured.
The National Games Organising Committee should expedite approval
of DPR, selection of sites, designs and detailed specification and
ensure completion of works on priority basis.

e Adequate staff should be posted for monitoring the activities of the
scheme.

e@ Internal control, inspection and monitoring mechanism should be
strengthen to ensure timely completion of sports infrastructure and
promotion of sports activities in the state.

MANI R AND PLANNING DEPARTM

॥ of Industrial Tr: [2 Institutes through Public

Private Partnership

2.6.1 Introduction

Technical Education contributes substantially to the socio-economic
development of the State and the Country as a whole. The Government of
India (GOT) decided to upgrade the Industrial Training Institutes (ITI) into
Centres for Excellence under Public-Private Partnership (PPP). Out of
118 ITIs in Chhattisgarh, 42 ITIs were selected for upgradation of existing
trades and commencement of new trades under PPP. As per the guidelines
issued (April 2008) by GOI, the main objective of the scheme is to improve
the employment outcomes of graduates from the vocational training system by
making design and delivery of training more demand responsive. The
guidelines further provided the nomination of an Industry Partner (IP) for
associating with the task of upgradation of ITIs. An Institute Management
Committee (IMC) as a registered society was required to be set up with the IP
as Chairman and the Principal of the ITI as the Member Secretary. The IMC
would prepare the Institute Development Plan (IDP) for development of the
ITL and submit the same to the State Steering Committee (SSC) to obtain the
loan from GOL. The Central Government has constituted a National Steering
Committee (NSC) with adequate representation from industry, State
Governments and other Central Government Departments to act as an apex
body for guiding implementation and monitoring of the scheme. The Secretary,
Manpower and Planning Department exercises overall control of the functioning
of ITIs in the State. Directorate of Employment and Training (DE&T) is
assisted by four! regional offices headed by Joint Director under whom
118 ITIs headed by Principals/ Training Superintendents are functioning in the
State. The audit of the scheme was carried out between April-July 2013
covering the period 2007-08 to 2012-13. We scrutinized the records related to
the scheme in DE&T, Raipur and the records of IMCs in 16 (Appendix 2.6.1)
out of 42 ITIs running under PPP Scheme.

Audit Findings

During audit of the records of test checked units, we noticed the following
irregularities:

2.6.2 Financial Management

As per the guidelines issued (April 2008) by GOI, an interest-free loan of
% 2.50 crore was to be given to each selected ITI by GOI for upgradation of
ITIs. The loan amount was to be kept in a public sector bank opened by the

IMC, For repayment of loan, there is a moratorium of 10 years from the year
in which the loan is released to the IMC. After the moratorium period is over,
the loan is payable by the IMC in equal annual installment over a period of
20 years. GOI will sanction and release the interest-free loans to the IMC only
on the basis of IDPs approved by the SSC.

An interest-free loan of = 105 crore was given to 42 ITIs selected under this
scheme by GOI. The detail of ITIs selected for upgradation, amount released

2.6.3 Revenue generation

As per Rule 55 of the Rules and Regulations of the IMC of ITI, the funds of
the Society shall consist of the interest free loan, income from investments and
savings, receipts by way of fees and charges for any short term or long term
training courses run by the Society in the ITIs, income earned by running
production/service centres by the Society in the ITIs, charges for consultancy
services provided by the ITI, Grants, contributions and donations received by
the Society and extra charges allowed to be collected by the State Government
to the Society for any paid seats in the ITI for any training course.

We noticed that the test checked ITIs had earned revenue of % 9.89 crore”
during 2007-08 to 2012-13. Nearly 90 per cent of revenue was from interest
earned on the fund available for upgradation of ITIs. While the [TL (Women)
Bilaspur had earned revenue of हैं 34.56 lakh, the rest of the 15 test checked
ITIs together had earned only = 18 lakh by way of tution fees from various
short term courses. The low revenue generation was mainly due to non
commencement of new trade and failure to upgrade the existing trade as per
the IDP as discussed in the subsequent paragraph. Considering the negligible
revenue earned by the ITIs, the possibility of repayment of the loan after the

Interest & 8.77 crore, other revenue हैं 1.12 crore

expiry of the moratorium period appears to be very remote and the ITIs need
to run short term courses for augmentation of revenue.

The State Government should take constructive measure for revenue
generation to ensure repayment of loan as and when due.

1 Non implementation of IDP lea to blockage of fund

As per Para 7 of the guidelines, Institute Development Plan (IDP) was to be
developed by the IMC in such a way that it leads to upgradation of the ITIs 88
a whole. Simultaneously, upgradation in a particular trade may also be taken
up. The IDP defines the long term goals of the ITI and details of the financial
requirement (Appendix 2.6.1) with year-wise break up to meet the needs.

The guidelines further provided that the 107 was to be submitted to the SSC
and after scrutiny, the same would be forwarded to Gol for release of funds.
Scrutiny of records of test checked ITIs revealed that the IDPs were sent in
anticipation of approval of SSC directly to National Steering Committee
(NSC) by the Department. In 16 test-checked ITIs, the IMCs had received a
total loan of € 40 crore during 2007-08 to 2009-10. The IMCs earned interest
of % 8.77 crore on the deposit made in the banks and FDRs during the last six
years. Out of the total available funds of % 49.89 crore (Appendix 2.6.2),
% 11.37 crore was utilised on purchase of tools and equipments, furniture and
fittings and additional manpower etc. and the balance funds amounting to
र₹ 38.52 crore remained unutilised for two to six years due to the following
Teasons:

e As per IDP, the five year plan was made to utilise the amount for starting

new trade after obtaining the affiliation of the course from National
Council on Vocational Training (NCVT) to improve the employment
outcome of graduates. For affiliation of new trade, a committee is
nominated by NCVT to ensure the standards and uniformity of training in
ITIs such as syllabi, tools and equipments, space, faculty etc. On
satisfactory inspection, the affiliation is granted.
Scrutiny of records revealed that the test checked ITIs had made a plan in
IDP to start 47 new trades (Appendix 2.6.3) to augment 1444 skilled
persons each year. As against this, only one ITI Le. ITI, Pali started new
trade as per plan in IDP while ITI, Jagadalpur and Mahasamund started
three trades against six trades planned by them. Remaining 13 ITIs had not
even applied for affiliation of new trade with NCVT even after completion
of two to six years since inception of the scheme. These ITIs could provide
only 176 skilled workers (12 per cent).

e@ For upgradation of existing trade (which should be affiliated to NCVT),
ITIs are required to procure tools and equipments. It was observed that
14 out of 16 test checked ITIs (Appendix 2.6.4) had made provision in IDP
for upgradation of existing trade but only two ITIs* had purchased tools
and equipments as required under the syllabus for upgradation of existing
trades. The remaining 12 ITIs had not procured complete set of tools and

Pali and Mahasamund

140


Chapter 2:Performance Audit

equipments even though funds were available. Thus the students were
deprived of the facilities and funds remained idle.

e@ IDP was prepared for a period of five years on the basis of which loan was
released by GOL. It was observed that within the five year period, the IDPs
of five’ ITIs were revised due to non initiation of programmes of original
IDP and three ITIs° had planned to change the original IDPs. Thus, due to
improper planning during the preparation of IDPs, the same had to be
frequently revised/changed which adversely affected the implementation
of the scheme.

On this being pointed out in audit, Principals of five ITIs stated (April-July
2013) that the new trades would be started after shifting to new building and
Principals of eight’ ITIs stated that after affiliation with NCVT new trade will
be started. For upgradation of existing trade, the Principals stated that the
deficient tools and equipments would be purchased soon.

The reply confirms that non adherence to the five year plan of IDP, non-
commencement of new trade and failure to upgrade the existing trade as per
IDP led to non-achievement of the objectives of the scheme

The State Government should streamline the planning process to achieve the
objective of the Scheme.

As per Para 5 of the scheme guidelines, the administrative control of the staff
of the [TI remains with the State Government and it will continue to pay their
salaries and other emoluments. The State Government is required to ensure
that the sanctioned strength of the instructors in the ITI is always filled up and
in no case, the vacancies exceed 10 per cent of the sanctioned strength at any
point of time. Further, all additional posts required by the ITIs are to be
sanctioned and filled up on priority.

Clause E of the scheme guidelines under annexure-iv provides to monitor
implementation of the Scheme at the State level, the State Government is
required to set up a State Steering Committee (SSC) with adequate
representation from the Industries. The SSC was assisted in management,
monitoring and evaluation of the Scheme by State Implementation Cell (SIC)
at State level and Governing Council® in each ITI. According to the guideline,
at least one meeting of SSC should be held in two months. We observed
various deficiencies in monitoring of the scheme as described below:

®@ We noticed that the SSC constituted for scheme “Centre of Excellence”
formed by the State Government in August 2007 was nominated as SSC
for this scheme also and first mecting of SSC was held in April 2011 and
after that, two more meetings were held (May 2012 and April 2013).

Governing Council consist of a representative nominated by the IP as the Chairman, four
other members trom local industries nominated by the State Government, the principal of
the ITI, is the ex-officio Member Secretary

142


thapter 2:Performance Audit

Thus, only three meetings were conducted upto April 2013 against
required 12 meetings (bi-monthly) as per the guideline. The IDP of 16 ITIs
were not placed before SSC for approval and the same was forwarded to
GOI by Department in anticipation of approval of SSC. In absence of
regular meeting of SSC, the progress of the scheme could not be
monitored and hence timely action could not be initiated for avoiding the
delay in implementation of the scheme.

© For management, monitoring and evaluation of the scheme at the State
level, a SIC was to be set up for a period of five years from 2007-08 to
2011-12. The STC was responsible for implementation of the Scheme
under the guidance of SSC. The office and administrative expenses
including salaries and other remuneration of the staff of the SIC would be
borne from the outlay of the Scheme. The SIC would be headed by the
State Director dealing with IT[s and may consist of full time officers
comprising of one Joint Director and two Deputy Director/Assistant
Director. For the above purpose, GOI sanctioned
र₹ 17 lakh (March 2010) for the financial year 2009-10.

We noticed that even afier implementation of the scheme, the SIC was not
constituted as envisaged in the scheme and the work of SIC was allotted to
one of the Joint Director along with other regular work of DE&T. The non
formation of SIC resulted in non utilization of GOI funds of = 17 lakh and
also poor monitoring of implementation of the scheme.

@ Para 40 of rules and regulations of IMC provides that the meetings of the
Governing Council should be held as frequently as is considered necessary
by the Chairman but in any case not less than one meeting in every three
months should be held. However, during the first one year of formation of
the Society when the process of upgradation of the ITI is underway, the
Governing Council is expected to meet once in every month.

We noticed that while ITI, Hathband, ITI (Women), Bilaspur and ITI,
Dhamtari had conducted the required meeting regularly as per the
guidelines, Seven ITIs’ had however organized less than 10 meeting and
six’ ITIs had organized 10 to 12 meetings during the last five year.

It was observed that there was poor internal control system and inadequate
monitoring mechanism to watch over the functioning of the ITIs. The
misappropriation of funds in ITI Mohala discussed below could have been
avoided had the Goveming Council met regularly to monitor the
implementation of schemes.

According to the guidelines of the scheme, the joint signature of the Secretary
of the IMC and the Chairman was mandatory for withdrawal of funds from the
bank.

During scrutiny of records of ITI, Mohala, it was observed that Vishal Iron &
Steel Corporation was selected as Industry Partner (IP) for Mohala ITI and
Managing Director of the company was nominated as the Chairman of the

IMC. An interest free loan of % 2.50 crore was released (September 2009) to

It was noticed that the Secretary requested (December 2010) the Bank to

signature of the Secretary. Even though, it was observed that withdrawals
amounting to = 12.03 lakh were made from the bank account during the period
from 31 December 2010 to 12 March 2012.

Further the Cash book, Bank Pass Books, Stock Registers, bills and vouchers
were not available with the Secretary. The annual books of account was
prepared upto 31 March 2010 only. Due to non-availability of the above
records, the Secretary could not forward the Quarterly Progress Reports (QPR)
to the DE&T, Raipur after December 2010.

Since no book of accounts, bills and vouchers were maintained in ITI, Mohala,
we compiled the fund position from bank statements and the QPR (December
2010) and noticed that out of the total receipt of € 2.54 crore in three bank
accounts of IMC, ITI Mohala, हैं 1.04 crore was shown as expenditure in the
QPR, & 1.13 crore was available in Fixed Deposit/ Saving bank accounts,
while no information was available on the remaining amount of & 36.70 lakh
On this being pointed out, the Secretary stated (July 2013) that three Bank
accounts were opened for [MC on the initiation of the Chairman. Further, it
was stated that records related to the IMC such as Cash Book, Cheque Book,
Pass Book, Stock registers, Annual accounts, etc were kept with the Chairman
without his knowledge. It was also stated by the Secretary that as all the
records were available with the Chairman, the information regarding the funds
could be provided by the Chairman only.

The reply of the Secretary is indicative of the fact that the Chairman had been
drawing the funds from the [MC accounts without the joint signature of the
Secretary and the detailed records of the funds were not maintained by the
IMC.

In this regard a departmental inquiry was initiated by a committee headed by
the District Collector and accordingly the department had requested
(September 2012) the Registrar of Firms and Society, Raipur to dissolve the
IMC of ITI Mohala. It was also noticed that a First Information Report (FIR)
was lodged against the Chairman of IMC Mohala for financial irregularities.
Thus, absence of regular meeting of SSC improper management and
monitoring of the scheme and weak internal control mechanism led to above
mentioned irregularity.

Department may develop policy for internal control and monitoring
mechanism for effective implementation of scheme.

Chapter 2:Performance Audit

The Key Performance Indicators (KPIs) are the yearly targets for next five
years, for improving the internal as well as external efficiency of the ITL The
KPls includes percentage of applications received as compared to number of
seats, enrolments as compared to number of seat, dropout as compared to
number of enrolments, passed out students employed/self employed within
one year of passout and average monthly income of the employed/self
employed students. These parameters are used to evaluate the success of the
scheme during and after the project period. The agreed KPIs signed by the
IMC and the State Government are appended to the Memorandum of
Agreement. We noticed that in 14 ITIs,'' the status of KPIs for the years
2007-08 to 2012-13 were as follows:

e During this period, out of total 44,890 applications received, only
11,907 (27 per cent) enrolments were made. Though the ITIs were
selected for upgradation, no plan was made to increase the enrolment of
students.

@ While percentage of the students passed out was more than 90 in
three ITIs'”, the same was between 40 and 89 in the remaining 10 ITIs.

@ None of the test checked ITIs maintained the basic database on
employment and average monthly income of pass out students. In absence
of this database, the Key Performance indicators as yearly targets for next
five years for improving the internal as well as external efficiency of the
ITIs could not be assessed.

Conclusion

The main objective of the scheme to augment the skilled work force through
upgradation of existing trade and commencement of new trades in ITIs under
PPP mode were not achieved. The infrastructure required for upgradation and
commencement of the new trade were not developed and affiliation could not
be obtained leading to poor performance of the scheme. Financial
mismanagement such as blockage of funds, and improper maintenance of
records were noticed. Institute development Plans were not adhered to by
IMCs. The scheme failed to take off due to shortfalls in infrastructure and
absence of regular faculties. Moreover, no steps were taken by the IMCs for
revenue generation as envisaged in the scheme. Deficiencies in
implementation of the schemes could not be identified and set right due to
weak internal control, improper monitoring and absence of regular meeting by
State Implementation Cell.

DE MEN’

g of Pre-Matri Hostels and Ashrams

2.7.1 Introduction

With an aim to bring the Schedule Caste and Scheduled Tribe (SC/ST)
children to schools from remote areas and to continue their studies, State
Government has formulated the Hostels and Ashrams Rules 2010. As per the
rules, hostels and residential schools (Ashrams) were to be set up with
provisions of minimum basic facilities like fresh drinking water, clean toilets
and bathrooms, fortnightly health checkups, free accommodation with
electricity, cots, mattress, blankets, mosquito net and utensils for cooking etc.
and schooling facilities. The Department of Schedule Caste and Scheduled
Tribe Development is responsible for implementation of the Rules, as well as
for providing computer training and special coaching to SC and ST students of
class 6" to 10" on difficult subjects at Hostels and Ashrams after school hours.
The Commissioner, Scheduled Tribes and Scheduled Castes development (CTD),
who implements the schemes, is assisted by the Director, Additional Directors
and Deputy Commissioners at the State level, Assistant Commissioners, Tribal
Development (ACTD) at the district level, Block Education Officers (850) at the
block level and Superintendents at Hostels/ Ashrams. We reviewed the
implementation of the scheme to see the adequacy of the basic infrastructure,
proper security arrangements and other facilities at Hostels and Ashrams.
There are 1254 Pre-Matric Tribal Hostels and 1153 Tribal Ashrams in the
State, of which 371 Hostels and 254 Ashrams covering eight! districts (two
districts from each division” out of 27 districts) were audited. The audit was
conducted through test check of records of ACTDs related to Pre-Matric Tribal
Hostels and Ashrams and field functionaries (Hostels and Ashrams) of the test
checked districts.

2.

.2 Budget and Expenditure

During 2008-13, the Department allotted € 274.47 crore for constructions of
Hostels and Ashrams, % 769.94 crore for providing basic facilities,
% seven crore for special coaching on difficult subjects and % 1.21 crore for
computer training at Hostels and Ashrams. Against the allotment, the
Department had incurred expenditure of = 271.29 crore on constructions of
Hostels and Ashrams, % 633.36 crore’ on providing basic facilities,
% 6.40 crore on special coaching on difficult subjects and = 7.30 crore on
computer training at Hostels and Ashrams.

Hostels and Ashrams which are running in rented or other Government
buildings and to take necessary steps to complete the construction of
incomplete buildings to ensure availability of basic infrastructure to the
students.

On this being pointed out, Assistant Commissioners, Tribal Development

bathrooms, Over head Tank in many Hostels and Ashrams was due to
functioning of these Hostels and Ashrams in rented/other Government

were issued to the Hostels and Ashrams Superintendents to utilise the
emergency light/lantem in case of non-availability of power failure as an
alternative facility

The fact remains that the basic needs of students of Hostels and Ashrams can
not be denied on the ground of their running in rented/other Government
buildings. The department should have made alternative arrangements for
providing the minimum basic facilities until they are shifted to their own
buildings. The reply furnished by ACDT, Rajnandgaon regarding utilisation of
emergency light/lantern is not acceptable as no budget provision was made
available to the Hostel Superintendents for alternative arrangement.

During discussion (December 2013), Secretary agreed to the observation
raised by the audit and stated that the lack of basic facilities like toilets,
bathrooms and Over Head Tanks was due to functioning of the Hostels and
Ashrams in Private and other Government buildings. After construction of
own building, these Hostels and Ashrams will be shifted to the own buildings
and the problem will be solved.

2.

Health check-up at Hostels and Ashrams

Under the Scheme of Swasth Tan Aur Swasth Mann, a fortnightly health check
up of all the students of Hostels and Ashrams was to be done through a doctor
of any District Hospital/Community Health Center/Primary Health Center.
However, where the Hostels and the Ashrams have no such facility, the check
up has to be done through any private practitioners.

Scrutiny of records revealed that fortnightly medical checkups were done in
353 (out of 625) Hostels and Ashrams either through private doctors or from
nearest PHCs. The students of remaining 272 Hostels and Ashrams were not
provided the benefit of fortnightly health check during 2008-13.

However as an exception, as evident from Table 2.7.2, in Dantewada district
however despite being an interior as well as Naxal affected area, the health
checkups in Hostels were undertaken at regular intervals.

On this being pointed out in audit, the ACTDs, Baloda Bazar, Dantewada and
Surajpur stated (May-July 2013) that the letter would be issued to Health
Department for regular check up of students of the Hostels and Ashrams.

medical checkups to hostellers is under process. ACTDs, Bilaspur, Kanker and

Ashrams through private doctors.
